
Prologue

From the case files of Michael Moast, cabinet 19, drawer 2, folder 8, entitled  "Origin of the Fractured Pot":

It happened in the old days.  The age of technology.

The world in those days was pressurized with tension - like an overripe melon about to burst.  World superpowers glared at one another from across (and within) the Earth's great seas.  Ghastly weapons bristled every shore.  Nasties of all kinds, so insidious that they that should never have even been dreamt of, let alone built.  

But built, they were, and in staggering numbers! 

Dictators, benevolent and otherwise, amassed great hordes of them.

It was one big powder keg.  All it needed was a spark -- one leader, more desperate and diabolical than the rest, to gauge the situation, to see the weak link, and to act.  A man like that could knock over the whole house of cards with but the slightest breath and set himself up as master above it all.  Yes, the time was indeed ripe for a bid for world domination.  

Tyrants had arisen many times before, but now the pieces were in place for it to happen on a truly global scale.  In the past, innocent people could run, or hide, or immigrate, or defect.  This time there would be no place to run and no place to hide.  In such a universal oppression, there would be no hope of escape in any form.  There could be nothing besides neverending serfdom... or death.  To the cruel dictator in charge, it would matter little which.

Looking back it's easy to see, just as looking back at the Nazis rise to power makes it seem so obvious what was about to happen.  But those who live in such times are always so strangely blind to things going down right before their very eyes.  Blind to their freedoms disappearing one by one.  Blind to seeing what it all pointed towards.

The tyrant in question was not hypothetical like some logic exercise.  He was a real man, who really existed and indeed his minions were already in place!  The hammer blow was about to fall.  The clock was ticking off the final seconds.  And there was only one person, the unlikeliest of heroes, who could change the outcome.

As surely as these good days were built upon the ruins of those, so it is for all of history.  This adventure begins, not in the age of technology, but in a time thousands of years prior, with a mighty people, both ancient and forgotten...


~1~
Volcano!

The small dog barked and ran from its master yet again.

"Ilíthio!" (fool!) she shouted.

Persephone chased after her pet.  She couldn't imagine what had gotten into him.  He was only a puppy still, but was usually so calm and docile.

As she chased the dog out into the stone-paved street she paused to take a deep breath, basking in the atmosphere of the greatest city-state the world had ever known.  The single island was a whopping 78 thousand square miles of lush jungle and fertile fields. (or, slightly larger than the state of South Dakota in today's terms.)  

Miles from the sea rose the glorious Mount Atlas, said to be the birthplace of the great god of the same name -- son of Poseidon the sea god.  Indeed, Atlas proved a mighty deity for the race that bore his name extended its mighty empire to many distant and unheard of shores.

Not far to the Northeast was another island, nowhere near as important on the world scene -- nearly irrelevant, in fact.  The tribal people who dwelt there were little more than barbarians.  The mere thought that it could ever make anything of itself, let alone become a world superpower was laughable in the extreme.

As the girl continued to try to soothe the frightened animal, little did she know that her invincible homeland was on the precipice of passing from fame into myth -- blotted out from the pages of history.  One day the lesser island would indeed rise to prominence.

But why would she have thought such a thing?  It was 425BC and in that day this was the capitol city of the Earth's mightiest empire.  It had fought and won battles on a hundred foreign shores.  Trade was booming.  On every street corner prosperity reigned.  

The mere thought that it could all be over in a blink?  Of course not!  They were immune.  This nation would endure to rule the ages!  (And such has been the thought of every great nation before or since - even right up to the moment of their very demise.)

Persephone ran through the stone streets of the bustling metropolis.  She darted between people with rich robes and servants swarming to and fro.  Most carried pots or baskets balanced on their heads full of goods either to be sold or recently bought.

All the while the small dog was just out of reach, taunting her ever onward.

She nearly cornered the wayward pup in a shallow alcove when she froze.  She felt something ripple through her body- a strange and terrifying energy that she had never felt before.

Her friend Dionesys the clothing merchant stepped out of his shop across the road.  He felt it too, but didn't know what to make of it either.  He glanced toward the towering Mount Atlas near the center of their majestic island.  It began to smolder, releasing a growing column of smoke into the serene sky.

The titanic energy continued unabated.  Soon the very ground began to swell and undulate beneath Persephone's feet like she was standing on the sea.  

The violence increased as she watched the transfixed shopkeeper staring at the distant mountain.  The man was so paralyzed with fear that he didn't see the stone facade of his own store begin to crumble, until it was too late, and he lay crushed like an insect beneath it.

The brutal horror shocked Persephone into action.  She scooped up her dog with one arm and ran as the sky slowly became darker.  She wasn't an expert on modern vulcanology, but could clearly see that the source of the disturbance was the mountain in the distance.  As if to confirm this, it began to hurl red hot stones into the sky.  They crashed down around her and the growing throng stampeding their way towards the sea, spurred on with the underworld smell of sulphur.

A man in an irrational headlong rush collided with her, sending her crashing into a streetside pottery vendor's wooden table.  Like the clothing merchant before, he too seemed frozen behind it, not even registering that his entire inventory was just dashed to pieces on the cobblestones.

Persephone lay dazed a moment next to a large orange pot that had split in two during the fall.  The crack ran right through the image of a woman who looked odiously like her.  

Her bruised head seemed slow to react.  Everything seemed hazy and far away.

She realized with a start that the haze wasn't a result of the head injury but was physically real in nature.  It burned her eyes and stung her lungs.  She tried to stand, but fell back again.  Something was broken.

She turned and was horrified to see a wall of scalding ash barreling down the street like a dam suddenly burst.

She wedged her body beneath the overturned potter's table in a feeble, last ditch attempt at saving her own life.

An offshoot of the coming wave of death rammed itself through a side alley to her left, creating a high-powered jet of fire, steam, and stone which carried her off the ground, clutching for dear life to the table.

She disappeared within the flame.


Those that survived the mobs, earthquakes, falling debris, scalding ash, and poisonous gas all found themselves at the seashore looking dumbly back and forth at one another. 
Now they had an even more devastating problem on their hands.  The marina was gone.  All of the docks sank beneath the sea along with all of the ships that weren't able to cast loose in time.  

The rest were making for open water as fast as sail and oar could take them. 

That left the fugitives with nowhere to flee.

The volcano thundered in the inky, flame-streaked sky.  It thundered like the malevolent laugh of some demon whose clutches could not be escaped.

Lava, which had begun to flow some time earlier, now ran freely down the streets.  Though deadly in its own right, it was slow enough to be avoided, and the townsfolk had a far more urgent problem on their hands.  The sea was rising faster and faster.  Many hurled themselves into it rather than return to the death-trap of the city.  Others, more wisely, took to the sea on trees and planks and anything else they could find that would float.

Even those, however, soon found that their time was up.  As they drifted in the water, they knew their fate was sealed when the great volcano itself exploded in a tectonic fireball.  The ear-splitting crack reached them a second or two later, but as it did, the sea turned into a giant black cavern.  It was like a starving black mouth that gobbled up the volcano, the village, the rest of the island, and any who were near enough to see it happen.

All of it, every last morsel, was slurped up by the hungry sea.  And then, having filled its belly, the ravenous deep closed its mouth once more.

The sea breeze began to dissipate the smoke.  

Soon growing patches of blue sky began to peer through the clouds once more.

In less than two hours' time all that was left of one of the world's greatest civilizations was nothing but a black smudge drifting on the surface of the sea - a steaming cauldron, slowly boiling itself out in the vast expanse of the great ocean.


A pair of Moroccan fishermen began hauling in their nets.  It had now been some days since a devastating tsunami of unknown origin wreaked havoc with the local sea life, and the fishing was only now showing the first signs of recovery.

One man glanced casually over the side of the boat.  He did it a hundred times a day and always saw nothing but water.  This time his heart leapt with a start as he spied the form of a human body floating atop the remains of a wooden table.  At least the overall shape was human enough, but in detail it looked more like a slab of roasted meat.

At first he thought the person must surely be dead, but she stirred, rolling slightly to one side.  There beneath the broken body of its owner peeked the dark eyes of a small, terrified puppy.


~2~
A New Beginning

The sun sank low over the Pacific, boiling away into orange lava.  Janet Jones stood at the edge of the surf watching it slowly disappear.  Her face was travel-worn and contemplative.  

A gentle sea breeze tossed pleasantly at her lightweight ankle-length skirt.  She breathed deep its salty fragrance as if it was what she'd come for.

Who knows?  Perhaps it was.

Janet, or "Jane" as she preferred to be called, was eighteen years old with tanned skin and with a slight hint of freckles.

She also had long, blond hair -- but not the soft, glamorous Hollywood kind of blond that other girls envied though.   Hers was more of a thick and course kind of blond, and far too yellow.  Being pulled back into a pony tail as it was, it resembled a stack of straw that had hitched a ride with her from the great plains.

Behind her on the beach was a white Toyota hatchback, out of gas, and probably stuck in the sand.

It didn't matter.  She'd decided to drive West, and this was as West as it gets.  Very literally, the end of the road.

The sun she'd chased for so many days kept on with its Westward journey, but for her, this was where she and her heavenly traveling companion would have to part company.

What did she hope to find on the road anyway?  Adventure, perhaps?
This was a very nice, but also very empty beach.  

Escape from her troubles?  
Closer.  But they seemed to shout so loudly behind her that they could be heard even here.  

Even now, she pulled out a bottle of prescription anxiety pills with her name on it.  She took two without water. 

A new start, then?
Yes!  That was it!
Time to forget it all.  Time to start over from scratch and make a new life - a better life!  This time could work out.  No.  Scratch that.  This time would work out.  She'd see to it that it did!

She bolstered herself with a another deep breath and set her chin.  She turned to face the nearby town of Santa Augusta California on the overlooking headland.  Its windows reflected sparkling gold back at her.  This would have to be it, then.

She followed her feet into the new future awaiting...

~3~
New Girl in Town
It was Sunday.  Her Aunt had always taken her to church, and so, to church she went.

No,  it was more than that.  If Jane had anybody left in life, it was Jesus.  That was where she had to start.  Starting over like this was much like making an unknown recipe - one where all you knew is that you needed flour.  You couldn't do much, but you could go to the store for some flour.  And that's at least a step in the right direction.

After searching the phonebook and placing a few calls to area churches, she decided on a few candidates.

Jane recalled the time her highschool history teacher had given the class a lesson on the Jim Jones cult.  From the way he had first described it, it sounded like a great place to be.  They did a lot of good deeds like feeding the poor, helping out hospitals, and cultivating inter-racial friendship in an age when segregation was still the order of the day.  

Her teacher had called for a show of hands, how many people wanted to be a part of a group like that?  Most hands went up.  

He then went on to describe how the group had communist ties, extorted its own people, and eventually poisoned over 900 of them to death - including 330 children!

She violently shook her head, trying to get her vivid imagination to let go of the revolting picture it was showing her.

In Kansas, the stories sounded like another world, but here in the middle of the California coast... that cult had risen to power only about three or four hours North of where Jane now stood!  To say she was extremely cautious was an understatement.
With the heart of man still the same now as then, coupled with the fact that the city was a complete unknown to her, Jane found herself with a pen and paper in hand.  Calling to mind all that she had been taught about what a healthy church should look like, she focused her thoughts and began writing a list:
Was the Bible the source of their teachings, or did they preach their own opinions instead?  
Was Jesus center stage, or was it some larger-than-life leader they revered?  
Were they permissive of whatever the world's sin dujour,  legalistically unforgiving, or did they have the Biblical balance of "preaching the Truth in love"?  
Did they act superior to all others, or as bankrupt sinners saved only by the grace of God?
Jane narrowed it down to a short list of places to visit in turn.  It was all too common for a website or church secretary to know all the right words to say.  Like the Jim Jones cult, who cares what a group claims they are?  What they really are is what's important!

That morning she hit up a cute Pentecostal church that was near the outskirts of town.  It was small and pleasant and Jane had nothing bad to say.  She had time to visit that and also catch a large downtown church's late service and thereby cross two candidates off her todo list in one day.

The downtown church was called Santa Augusta Church of Life, a non-denominational evangelical group set up in a renovated warehouse.  The place was a hive of commotion and borderline madness that morning.  A lot of people introduced themselves to her, but it was hard to say whether that was out of friendliness or whether the close proximity the tightly-packed lobby forced people together.

Was that forced camaraderie a result of exceptionally clever, or exceptionally poor, planning?  
Hard to say.

Either way, the crowd was becoming a bit too much and her head began to feel a little dizzy.  

In the medical vernacular, Jane had something called Asperger's Syndrome, a milder form of Autism.  In more practical terms, it meant that she had super-acute senses, which all tried to cram their information into her mind at once causing the equivalent of Los Angeles rush hour traffic on the way to her brain.  If it got too bad, nothing would make sense at all anymore and she'd have a breakdown.  You could call it 'Information overload', to put it mildly, but hers could be brought on from what most other people would see as a normal, everyday situation.  A simple crowd of people with fifty different conversations meant fifty different conversations that her mind was trying to understand all at once.  Add to that movement, colors, the smell of perfume...

She clutched her sternum and took a slow, deep breath.  Even the thought of it was making her head swim and the room seem to spin around her.  

She pulled out her pill bottle and hit it at the maximum the dose would allow.  She hated to.  Each time the level went lower, she knew, it pushed her that much closer to the end.  There were still many pills left, but she feared they would disappear far too fast.  Then it would mean trying to find a new psychiatrist who would willingly give her more and yet not ask about her past.  This unlikely combination of traits made the problem insoluble.  The dilemma was a dark cloud that continued to loom on her horizon.

As she stood near the sidelines waiting for a sense of normalcy to return, Jane was approached by a deliberately friendly greeter named Tim.  He introduced himself as such even through his name tag clearly said it as well.  "Tim", it said in sharpie.  And there was a smiley face.

He gave her a whirlwind tour of the bustling building, answering her questions the best he could.

"It's a little busy here.  Don't you get lost?" Jane asked.

"What?"  (The ironic part was that he wasn't even trying to be ironic.)

She repeated the question at a near shout.

"Oh, yeah!  It's easy to get lost.  That's why we encourage everybody to be a part of one of our Bible Study small groups."

He picked up a two-page folded document off a nearby table.

"Here's a list." said Tim.  "You can check out whatever appeals to you, but we encourage people to find one and stick with it - forges relationships and all that good stuff."

The sound of a guitar and muffled singing began echoing throughout the building.  A wave of last-minute chit-chatters headed for the doors to the main auditorium.  That group included Jane and Tim, Smileyface.  The latter seemed convinced that it was his sworn duty to find her a seat against all odds.

After the remaining half of the song was over, she sat and listened to a variety of announcements (many of which did not apply to her).  As she did, she glimpsed absently over the list of Bible Studies.

One, in particular, caught her attention:
Singles of all ages
Childcare: none
Looking for Photography Enthusiasts

Interesting...

Had she remembered to grab her camera in her haste to leave Ditchwater Kansas?

She had!  Jane always loved the art of photography, so maybe this was a way to find common ground and get to know people.

And "single" people?  Who knows?  Maybe she could find a guy to boot - although that thought was a fleeting one.  She knew her life was crazy as a bag of cats at the moment.  She had a long way to go before she even started looking down that road.   

As the service concluded the maelstrom blew into the hallways once more,  Jane realized that she'd probably just found the church she'd wind up with by the time she reached the end of her list.  

It was a lot bigger and more chaotic than anything she'd been used to, but they passed all of the qualifications to be considered legit Christians.  Given that, the only remaining tie-breaker would be where she would personally fit in the best.  The Bible Study seemed like it might fulfill that need.

" 'Photography Enthusiasts'..." she mused.  Then smiled.


~4~
Jack

The woman wandered around the Virginia Beach waterfront plaza with a worried look on her face.  Her sun visor and flowered Hawaiian shirt gave her the aspect of a tourist.  Her foreign-sounding California accent only amplified the image.

She'd made the rounds all morning, asking desperately each person whether they knew the directions to the Lillian Edwards Convention Center.  Nobody had yet been able to direct her to the location.  Neither the local clerks at the souvenir shops, nor the wait staff at the various sidewalk restaurants could point her in the right direction.

Many of the employees watched her saga with great sympathy as they bussed tables or waited on customers.  A few even took the quest a step further and attempted to search the phone book or use the web to help her out.  Still, it all availed nothing.

Perhaps it had something to do with the fact that the Lillian Edwards Convention Center did not actually exist.  And the woman knew it, too!

She spied a bent-over old lady pushing an antique pram down the sidewalk.  It was no secret to anyone watching that her quest was getting desperate.  That part was no act from her perspective either.

The buggy's wheel squeaked softly as the old woman shuffled along behind it.

"Excuse me, ma'am.  Do you know the way to the Lillian Edwards Convention Center?"

The lady looked up.  Her grey hair was disheveled and seemed to not fit her head properly.  The face was ruddy and there was even a trace of a five o' clock shadow.

"Do you have a map of Oklahoma City?" she croaked.  It was a frustrating and silly thing for the old lady to ask.

But lo and behold, the woman did have one!  She handed it over and the old lady unfolded it. 

Here and there, intermixed with the street names, were additional words written in blue ballpoint ink.  She made a superficial adjustment of her bifocals and examined the map closely.  Her finger traced from location to location, stopping at certain blue words and skipping others entirely.

She took the map and held it even closer to her face.  So close, in fact, that nobody in the plaza could see her lips when they began to move.

"This is impossible Melinda!" the old woman said, now in a soft man's voice.

"Seems that way.  But it couldn't be as bad as--"

The old women wrinkled her nose and growled, "Atlantis."  

"Yeah, well..."  Melinda brushed off the painful reference and continued on regardless, "Grab Kurt or Wendell as your backup.  If this conspiracy thing is for real, then it's way too big for you to handle on your own."

"Do I get a starting location, or do I just have to wing it?"

"Take the map.  It's all there in standard up-3 encoding." she said.

And after a pause, "But here's a quick spoiler for you Jack:  It's smack-dab in the middle of Siberia."

Jack rolled her eyes.  "Siberia huh?  Lucky me."

Melinda looked into the pram.  It contained a lovingly-swaddled Nerf football.  "By the way, that is the ugliest baby I've ever seen."

"Takes after his grandpa."

Melinda's sudden laugh nearly blew her cover.

"Sorry 'bout that.  You be careful out there.  Got it?"

Jack, the old woman, nodded.

Melinda stood up straighter.  She smiled and said loudly for the crowd's benefit.  "Thank you very much!  I knew convention center was around here somewhere!"

The old lady shook the grateful hand.  She resumed slowly pushing the carriage down the sidewalk in the original direction she'd been heading.  She still held the map, but nobody seemed to question that.

"Siberia..." she muttered to himself.


~5~
Photography Enthusiasts

Jane checked the address in her hand one more last time against the gleaming brass numbers affixed to the gate.  Its white-painted wrought iron entwined in interesting spirals and swirls like some grand vine.

The guard in his little shack had seen her (recently resurrected) Toyota hatchback pull over.  He raised an eyebrow slightly, wondering at her business here.

Jane wondered about that too.  There was no other way to read the numbers on the paper she'd picked up at church that morning.  The address certainly belonged to the stately mansion behind the gate.

But why not?  Millionaires could volunteer to host a Bible Study just the same as anybody else.

The logic of that was sound, but did nothing to calm the sense of uneasiness presently doing backflips in her gut. 
A worried set of images and thoughts flashed across her mind.  If the stereotypes usually associated with the very rich were to be believed, she wanted to make absolutely sure she wasn't knocking on the wrong door and about to get the scolding of her life.
She frowned at the prospect of it.  Social interactions weren't exactly her forte.  (One trait that all those with Asperger's share.)

Instead of the direct approach, she came up with an alternate plan.  She'd talk to the guard and act like she was lost.  Maybe it was silly, but at least it was a safe way to learn more without making the world's biggest fool of herself.

Jane walked over to the guard shack.  The man ("Carl", as the patch on his uniform announced) peered out of his narrow window.  He was a kind looking fellow with a broad smile and happy eyes.  The first traces of grey danced at the edges of his hair.  He was also built like a Freightliner and could easily afford to be happy, simply because there was nobody suicidal enough to make him un-happy.  

Everything about him screamed, 'retired linebacker' to one and all.  They would've been half right.  He was, indeed, retired, but from the position as the homeowner's right-hand man and most trusted business colleague.  Indeed, a multi-millionaire in his own right, he was more here out of loyalty to a friend than for need of a job.

"Excuse me..." she said.  She always went out of her way to be polite with strangers.  That went double for strangers that could physically throw her out, Toyota and all!

"Yes?"

"I'm looking for the Michael Moast residence."

"This is the Moast residence."

"Oh."  Well, that plan was over with, so... Now what?

Every emotion rising within her shouted, Run away!  Run away from the strange imposing building, run away from the huge guard, and go home and hide under the bed.  Pretty much, forever.

With sheer grit of determination, she resolved to ignore them and to continue anyway.  "Well, I was looking for this Bible Study and..."

Jane was holding up the paper from church as proof, but the man didn't even look.  He was too busy looking down at some unseen gate control as he fumbled to do -- whatever 'open sesame' magic he needed to do.  With the slight whir of an electric motor the gate began to swing aside to let her pass.

"Oh." said Jane again, a little surprised.  And then, "Thank you." as she turned and trotted off back to her car.  

She drove through and waved her thanks to Carl once more.  He waved back.

The drive was long and bordered with pine trees, manicured into ridiculously skinny, yet tall, cones.  Behind at least one she caught a glimpse of a hidden security guard, no doubt carefully watching the new stranger.

The pine-scented air hung immobile and still, making the crunch of her tires on the road sound like she was driving over corn flakes.

The drive terminated in a wide circular turnaround with a fountain in the middle of it.  In keeping with the house's scale, the fountain was huge as well.  Several slender jets shot inwards to a central pedestal which stood over five feet high.  It proudly bore a life-sized male lion roaring towards the sky in all his fierce majesty.  A geyser of water emanated from its mouth as if the roar had become liquid and then splashed loudly back into the frothy pool.  The turbulent noise of the fountain had a gushing roar all its own, which only served to reinforce the motif.
 
Several cars were parked around the circumference of the roundabout,  many of them looking quite expensive.  There were a pair of matching black Mercedes SUV's with all the upgrades, a red hot Ferrari, and an antique and interesting car that Jane called a Model T.  

(A car buff would have identified it as a rare specimen of a 1912 Stanley Motor Carriage Company Model 64 steam-driven roadster.  Or, perhaps by its more well-known slang term, a "Stanley Steamer".)

There were other cars there too.  Regular cars.  Cars like hers.  At that realization she breathed a major sigh of relief.  At least this was a real Bible Study with real people in it, and not some millionaires-only club!

She subconsciously parked next to a 'regular car' rather than one of the exotic ones.

Jane got out.  She looked at her camera in the back seat.  She didn't know whether they did their photography at the actual meeting or whether they just talked shop and shared their latest photos.  Just in case Jane finally decided to sling her camera around her neck and bring it along.

She walked up several steps to an ornate and imposing set of double doors.  The delicate molding work on and around the doors was quite beautiful but also made her feel all the more out of her element.  They seemed to loom above her like a pair of sentinels glaring down.

She still hadn't entirely let go of the option of running away and never coming back.  Before Jane's self-doubt could toy with that idea any more, she hurriedly pushed the brass door bell button.  

Inside a chime dong'ed out a complicated refrain of classical music that was difficult to place due to the sudden and frantic barking of some small dog within.

The sound of soft footsteps could be heard drawing nearer.  Following that were some unidentifiable mechanical noises that seemed to come from somewhere deep inside the door itself.

Jane half expected to see somebody named "Jeeves" answer the door.  Instead a man, tall and trim, in his mid 30's appeared.  The barking grew louder still as a small white poodle tore between his legs and charged at her in a bloodthirsty rage. 

She didn't flinch.  Why would she?  The greatest damage the tiny thing could even dream of doing would be to rip a hem or chew a shoelace.

"I'm here for the Bible Study." she explained, gesturing at the paper in her hand.

The man's face brightened slightly.  "Oh, I see.  Please come in." he replied.

And then to the dog, "Stop it Thor.  She's a guest."

Jane sniggered softly at hearing the dog's name.  The man didn't respond, so she stopped herself and stepped inside.

Inside the marble entryway, she noticed a bench with a disorderly pile of shoes about it.  

The shoes were all adult sizes and of assorted types that didn't really mesh with the presumed style of the owners.  She correctly deduced that there was a "no shoes" rule in effect.

As she sat down and removed her shoes, Jane again heard the series of strange mechanical noises once more.  This time it almost sounded like a cross between an airy swoosh and metal-on-metal shink noise like a sword being drawn.  The only thing it could have been was a locking mechanism, but judging by all the complex sounds involved, this house must have tighter defenses than Fort Knox!

The man turned around and saw her shoes off.

"Ah, yes.  Sorry.  Wife has a rule about that.  She says it wears out the ground."
He hesitated.
"I.. I mean 'floor'."  

It was odd that he felt the need to correct himself over that, which only drew attention to the fact that it was an odd way to phrase things to begin with.  Odder still, he now seemed almost ashamed of having said it.

"My name's Jane Jones." said Jane Jones.

"Michael Moast.  You can just call me Michael."

His hand shot out and she took it.  His hands were rough and the handshake was crushing -- not 'crushing' like a man out to prove himself, but more like a man who didn't know his own strength.

Michael Moast handed her a pair of house slippers and shrugged apologetically.  "I hope the shoe thing isn't an inconvenience.  If so, just keep them on and I'll deal with the Missus."

She shook her head.  It wasn't an inconvenience.  She soon found that he needn't have apologized anyway.  The slippers he'd given her were quite warm and almost scandalously soft.  In fact, they were so terribly comfortable Jane wondered if her feet would stage an all-out revolt rather than go back into their proper homes at the end of the evening.

They walked down a long corridor made of glossy Carrara marble.  Each side was lined with short Greek-style pillars.  They were also made of marble, and each was individually lit from above by its own tiny halogen spot light.  On top of every one them, where one would normally expect to see some rare antique or sculpture, was instead what seemed to be an entirely random object.

This man, Michel Moast, who identified himself as the owner of the home, talked fast and walked faster.

Jane wanted to examine or ask about the strange items lining the corridor, but they mostly blurred past.  Moreover, her host's chatter allowed no opportunity to get a word in edgewise, even if she could have done so without wheezing.  

Michel Moast was evidently very well-off in his mansion and chic Italian wool jacket.  Underneath its custom-tailored exterior he also had some serious muscles going on.  He was certainly no pencil-pushing yuppie.  This guy was tough as a coiled spring, from the way he moved.  

His eyes were also uncannily alert, darting this way and that, and not missing anything.  Overall, it seemed as if a lion could leap from a doorway at any moment and he'd be ready to wrestle it to the ground with his bare hands.  And woe unto business competitors that crossed his path!  In a boardroom he'd be equally as formidable!

Eventually they arrived at the far end of the passage where he stopped to open a pair of large white doors.  Jane was finally able to make out some of the mysterious items on the pillars.  She could see the last one on both sides plain and clear now.  One was a crumpled and torn Yale sweatshirt and the other was a badly mangled and corroded airplane landing gear.  

Strange.  
Very strange.

The doors opened into a combined kitchen and living room.  This 'great room' (as architects often called it) would have seemed quite large and quite nice in an average new suburban home.  But here, tucked behind a door in this immense palace, it seemed strangely small and out of place.  

The decor kept with the average-but-nice theme.  Wooden floor, recessed lighting and some plush leather sofas...  Even an inviting gas fireplace with a stone facade flickered away at one end of the room.

Although the seating area looked mighty inviting, it went unappreciated by all of the other guests who were standing around in the kitchen, chatting.

"Hello, everybody," said Michael Moast.  They stopped their various conversations and turned to look at him.  He had a naturally commanding voice that could not be ignored. "We have a new member of our group tonight, Miss Jane Jones."

"Look at that! Tarzan and Jane!" laughed a tall fellow in the back.  It was a little muffled because his mouth was full.

Jane mistakenly thought the jibe was addressed to her.  She had no time to feel offended though, as a large plastic ladle suddenly cracked the guy on the back of the head with a loud, pop!

The ladle was wielded by a short and somewhat chubby lady with kind, crinkles around her eyes.

Michael only rolled his eyes at the display.  "Okay, calm it down folks.  Everybody take turns.  Introduce the person to your right."  He rubbed his temples.  "Starting with the wise guy."

The fellow who'd made the crack immediately straightened himself.  Despite instructions, he introduced the person on his left -- the same Irish woman who was responsible for the ladle blow.

"This is Ms McCready.  She's in charge of snacks!"  He said it with great personal pride.  The recent violence was seemingly already forgotten in lieu of the elder woman's snacks.

She smiled warmly.

This 'Ms McCready's height was on the short side of average.  To go along with it, she was plump to the point of chubby, such that her elbows protruded in, rather than out when her arms were straight.  She had a kindly face.  A face that made you instantly like her.  

She also hovered around the kitchen as if she owned the place, giving some amount of credence to her much-touted snack making ability.

The direction change cause by the tall boy made it Ms McCready's turn to go next.  "Hello, dearie!" she said warmly. "I'm sorry t' say that Jack is not here tonight.  You'll have to meet him some other time.  And..."

She looked around, confused.

"Where's Kurt?" she asked.

"He's not here." said someone.

"How strange.  Kurt is always here." she explained.

"No.  We told you on the phone that Jack and Kurt are both... out of town.  They aren't going to be here tonight, remember?" said Michael Moast.

"Oh, of course!  Must t' forgot for a moment.  Y' know, me old memory ain't what she used t' be."

"Introductions?" he reminded her.

"Ah yes, standing to me left is, his very own self, the famous Dr Puttery."

Whether the compliment was supposed to be genuine or facetious was hard to say.  The recipient's reaction gave no indication of that either.  The words were not acknowledged, only absorbed.

The man was average height, average weight, with average hair that had a little graying around the ears suggested that he was getting on in years.  He lifted his drink in toast to the newcomer.  He always carried a drink in his hand, but its level never seemed to get any lower.  Almost like he carried it just to carry it rather than to drink it.

By all accounts his appearance was average.  He could have passed for anything from an insurance agent to a bus driver.  Yet that was all before one accounted for his clothing.

By far the strangest thing about the man was his silk pajamas.  It just lent a sharp, eccentric twist to his entire persona.  It would have seemed weird enough to see the owner of the house show up in his or her pajamas, but at least it would've actually been his house.  Who in the world would show up to somebody else's house in their pajamas?  It hinted of insanity.  And coupled with the "Dr" salutation, Jane thought he'd have made a great mad scientist.

(In a matter of weeks she'd find out how close her initial assessment really was!)

It was time for Dr Puttery to make his introduction, which he did with another raise of the glass, "Our hostess, Mrs Melinda Moast".  He gestured with his drink to the woman next to him.

The charming and finely-attired Melinda Moast broke off her conversation and smiled a flawless smile.  She was slender and prim.  Her hair was permed to perfection and bounced slightly as she spoke.  She wore a cream satin top, matching suit pant, and had a golden necklace strung with iridescent pearls.  They were most likely the real deal!

She wore wealth like somebody who was well accustomed to it.

Before speaking, Melinda yawned broadly.  "Sorry about that.  Just got off a long flight.

"I'm Micheal's wife.

"Next to me is Misty Carlisle."

The girl indicated was short.  Very short.  She wasn't five feet nor anywhere close to it.  Jane didn't know what the official line was that distinguished a person as being a 'dwarf', but this girl was probably in the running.  The small-sized stature was only enhanced by the fact that she wore a large mens' knit sweater.  The rest of her body must be in there somewhere.

Her soft voice matched her size perfectly.  "Now my turn, I guess?

"The tall guy who started us off is Weird Wendell."  The teasing nickname was intended to embarrass him, but the fact that she'd dared to use it made her blush instead.

She blushed, but he bowed, giving it plenty of regal ham.  "Wendell St Frank, at your service.  Man-about-town, doer of derring--"

"Eater of pizza."  added Melinda Moast.

"Wendell likes pizza."  Wendell confirmed with a grin.

To look at his face a person would instantly peg the guy as Japanese.  His drawn eyes agreed.  Even his black hair was trimmed in a stereotypical Japanese bowl cut.  What broke with the picture was his height.

Misty wasn't exaggerating when she described him as tall.  He topped out at nearly seven feet!  He was also skinny as a bean to go along with it.

"Well, I think that's everybody, then." said Michael Moast.  And then he made the executive decision that everybody was waiting on.  "Everybody take a seat and we'll get started."

As soon as everyone had gathered at the couches, they began the official gathering with prayer and then dove straight into their Bible Study.

At present, they were somewhere in the middle of the book of Deuteronomy - "Moses' Memoirs", as the study guide called it.

Jane was a little lost that evening, having come in during the middle of the series.  It took some time to come up to speed but soon she was able to contribute to the group discussion.

When it came to talking to the others, she felt a little conspicuous with whatever Midwestern accent she must have had.  To her ear it sounded 'normal' of course, but in the minds of these Californians with their unique rapidfire prattling accent it must have sounded foreign.  At least Ms McCready and her thick Irish tone made it so she wasn't the only one different in the room.

They fairly burned through the questions in Michael's leaders' booklet, leaving plenty of time for chatting afterwards.

All during the study Ms McCready drifted back and forth between the couches and the kitchenette.  Whatever she was up to in there smelled amazing!  Just as they were wrapping up, she pulled a pan full of piping hot cinnamon rolls out of the oven.  She placed it on top of the stove to cool.  She seemed a kind lady.

As everybody talked about... whatever everybody talked about, Jane's eyes made a circuit around the new surroundings of the room.  She noticed in the magazine rack next to the couch.  Intermixed with several issues of "Gourmet Living" magazine was a single copy of "Soldier of Fortune".  "Axyl Rabishaw: 'Don't call me a mercenary' ", read the headline article.  That was unexpected.  Even more unexpected was the total lack of any reading material even remotely related to photography.  Usually when people were interested in a hobby or sport you'd expect that they would have at least something related to it lying around.

The earlier observation was now compounded by a second clue.  On the mantle were several snapshots of the Moasts and even a few of the Bible Study group together.  None looked professionally taken.  In fact, short of seeing someone's thumb in the shot, they were almost perfectly awful.

It just didn't add up in her mind, so Jane decided to go fishing.  "So how long has everybody been into photography?"

Michael Moast's brow furrowed in confusion.  " 'Photography'?  None of us do professional photography."  He was trying to make any sense of her question and decided to be of assistance the best he could.  "But I can recommend a few people if you need somebody."

"But the card I got at church..."  Jane held it up.  "It says 'Photography Enthusiasts', right here."

All eyes visited the card and then turned toward Weird Wendell.

" 'Photography'!??" Melinda exclaimed, "I told you we needed advanced genetics!"

"Well, I can't exactly put that in a church bulletin, now can I?  I decided to make up a code."

Melinda did a full face-palm.  "Wendell, we've been through this.  It isn't a real code if nobody else knows what it is."

Wendell was speechless.  The best he could muster was a sheepish grin.

The strange interchange left Jane even more confused.  She glanced down at the camera hanging in front of her stomach.  It now seemed out of place at best.

Ms McCready noticed the situation starting to unravel and decided to jump in.  She quickly grabbed the pan off the stove top.

"Oh, don't mind those two sillies, dear.  Here.  Have the first cinni-."

She pried the sticky treat loose from the pan carefully, but quickly.  Almost too quickly.  The bun was already waiting in midair before Jane could grab a paper plate off the counter and slide it underneath.

"Be careful there.  Hot out of the oven.  I'll go get the frosting."

She returned in a moment grasping a sauce pan in her large oven mitt.  Slowly she drizzled the warm frosting over the bun and it immediately began to melt into the warm crust.

The delicious aroma drifting off her plate in a flowing curl of steam broke down any worries over the strange comment a moment earlier.  Its psiren call was too much to resist for long and before she new it, a warm fork full was already in her mouth.  The smell made no promises that the flavor didn't deliver in full.

Only when she held an empty plate with a sticky stain did Jane finally realize that she was rudely ignoring the rest of the group and their conversation.  She felt bad for a split second before realizing that there was no other conversation.  All of the rest of the group was tearing though cinnamon buns of their own.  All, except for Ms McCready, who was busy filling the sink to allow the empty, sugar-encrusted pan a chance to soak.  And then there was Wendell, who was long since finished, and trying desperately to lick every last morsel of flavor off his paper plate.

"Got any more of this, Ms M?" he asked.  

He saw the empty pan.  He knew the answer.  But still he asked, just on the off-hand chance there was some extra bun that the miracle-working cook could conjure out of thin air.

"Oh. 'fraid not." she replied, not turning around.

Jane almost flushed when she realized that the sweet old cook had counted on exactly one for everybody.  To make the numbers work out, she had given Jane her own!  The only thing that lessened the blush was that she knew that the nice lady would have probably even given it away to Weird Wendell if Jane wasn't there that night.

The evening wound down rapidly after the treats were no more.  Jane shook hands and hugged and said goodbye to all.  She promised them and herself that she would return next week.  

She really meant it too.  They weren't much as far as "Photography Enthusiasts" went, but were a great bunch nonetheless.


~6~
A Friendly Face

Jane's hands shook slightly as she rapped on the back service entrance door to the Blevens department store.  She was precisely on time for the job interview.  

She had plenty of time to reflect as she gazed at the metal door and its peeling white paint over bubbling orange rust.

She knew retail well from her old life, but without access to references from those days, she was essentially starting over.  It was a bitter pill, but starting over was at least starting.  And who knows?  Maybe she could work her way back up to assistant manager again.  Even in spite of still being considered young for such a position she'd accomplished it once before, so why not again?  In fact, it'd be all the easier this time, for she already knew the business.

"What?  What is it?" shouted an annoyed male voice inside.

It didn't sound compelled to answer the door.

Jane's only option was to shout back,  "Hello!  This is Janet Jones!  I have an interview!"

She heard a buzz and a click of the door's locking mechanism.  There was no verbal response to go with it, but she tried the handle.  Sure enough, the lock had released.  She opened it and stepped inside.

It opened into a stock room like any other.  The floor was concrete and row after row of grey metal shelves stood with their reserve supply of merchandise.  Dust motes wandered in and out of little beams of light streaming in from overhead.

"What is it?" said the grouchy voice again.  

She turned left toward its source.  There sat a dusty, over-piled steel desk.  This, in turn, sat next to a metal roll-up loading dock door where semi trailers could drop off their shipments.  It was an ugly military surplus desk with a cheap engraved name tag that read, Walter Blevens, Owner / Manager.

She wasn't sure whether the surly man didn't hear what she said when she was outside or whether he simply didn't care enough to listen.  She suspected the latter.  But it made no difference in the end.  

She walked over and offered her hand.  "Mister Blevens, I'm Janet Jones. I came about the stock clerk --"

He didn't take the hand, but cut her off.  

(Jane had been interviewed before and knew better than to sit without being offered to.  He never offered.)

"Do you sell?"

"But the job was --" she explained.

"I know what the job was," he said rudely, "But I asked, 'Do you sell?'"

"Yes, sir.  Very well, sir." she tried the super-politeness tactic.

It failed.

"Good.  I had a walk-off this morning.  'Kid couldn't take it.  You think you can?"

"Absolutely!  Thank you!"

"Don't thank me.  Show me.  You make me money, you got a job.  You don't, then don't let me see you back here again.  Got it?"

"Got it."

"Spiffy.  Sale's floor's that way."  he nodded towards a door on the far opposite wall.

The conversation was clearly over.

As she walked off in the direction indicated, Jane could easily imagine why 'the kid', had walked off.  The boss was about as warm and cuddly as an electric eel.

Still, she needed a job and, although Blevens was probably used to people being steamrolled by his toughness, she was sure he'd just met someone tougher.  She'd show him!  She'd show all the people of Ditchwater Kansas too!  She'd build a new life with her own bare hands!  Right here!  From the very dust if needs be!

The new-found determination lent her steps a brash, bold resolve.  She strode so decisively, having pumped herself up with all the self-confident thoughts, that she nearly ran over one of her new coworkers as she burst through the door onto the sales floor.

To Jane's amazement, the girl she'd almost flattened was the exact same girl from the Bible Study the night before.  'Misty'?  Yes!  That's what her name was!

"Hello Misty!  I didn't know you worked here!" said a stunned Jane.

"What're you doing here?" asked an equally stunned Misty in return.

"Mister Blevens said one of the salespersons just quit and I can have the job -- if I can prove I can sell, that is."

"Can you?" asked Misty.

"Well... yes.  If you wouldn't mind showing me around the place real quick." 

Jane thought a moment. "And also what I'm supposed to be selling might be good to know."

Misty smiled.

"Well, I'd assume you're replacing Adam then.  He sold menswear, but I think we might see if Ted wants to swap for Jewelry.  He gets so antsy cooped up behind that counter.  Then you'd be able to work with me and I can show you the ropes as we go."

"Thanks." said Jane, genuinely touched by her kindness.

Come to think of it, Jane's skills of noticing the smallest details in life also picked up on how fast Misty was able to pull such an ideal plan out of her hat.  She had all the marks of someone who could really think on her feet -- like someone who was used to quickly arriving at, and executing the right decision.  In short: one sharp cookie!

This initial assessment rang true.  As the day marched on and Jane started to catch on to company policy, the two excellent salesgirls began to develop into a strong team.  Many times the store was busy enough that they had to split up and sell individually.  Misty got her exercise during these times since she had to both do her own selling and unlock whatever cabinets Jane needed.  (Blevens would have skinned a person alive for giving the keys to a first-day trainee.)

Most of the time though, shoppers were scarce and the rare browser was all that showed up.  This was where the new team really distinguished itself.  Whenever an unwary passer-by hesitated to look at something that caught her eye, she was assuredly doomed.  Soon she would find herself and the two associates joking and laughing (and trying things on) like a trio of old friends.

Jane had learned long ago that people never bought 'a thing' itself, but instead they were buying the emotional perception that the item promised.  Therefore, the buying experience itself is often more important than the product being sold!

As the day came to an end, Jane rang out the register, gave her new friend a big hug and walked through the doors leading to the loading dock.  

Blevens sat at the same desk as that morning, poking at a grimy calculator with equally grimy fingers.  

Jane had experienced her share of difficult people in the past and knew the type.

For example, the troll was definitely not the sort to tolerate insubordination.  But when it came down to it, cold, hard cash would trump even that.

Jane was so certain she had the man pegged, that she strode confidently past his desk, dropping the long register tape on it as she breezed by.

"See ya tomorrow." she said without turning around.  And she was gone.


~7~
Agent 12

Midnight.

A faint light glowed from the window of the bridge house of the wrecked vessel.  It used to be a wooden boat of some kind, but now sat at an insane angle, still tied to the dock but half submerged.  The vessel didn't float on the water so much as it hung off the dock for its support.  There were only a few chips of paint still stubbornly clinging to the grey weathered wood, but not enough of any one color to guess which one the boat was last painted.

The dim illumination cast interesting, constantly-moving, puddles of light on the cats' paws of the still lagoon.

Within was an oriental-looking man crouched over a portable radio set, itself ancient and worn out as the boat.

To look at him, every part of the man's appearance screamed East-Asian sailor.  But his voice betrayed the look as it spoke into the ancient microphone with a perfect New York accent.

"Agent 12 calling USS-17701.  Agent 12 calling USS-17701.  Do you read?  ...Over."

There was no response besides static.  He repeated his call.

"Agent 12, this is Aunt Bea.  What news from Mayberry?" the grizzly voice came back.

"Aunt Bea, tell Opie to wash up.  It's past his bed time."  He bristled at having to use the ridiculous pass-code exchange.

With that behind him though, he got down to business.  "Have completed Draco surveillance.  Subject, name is Fontana, Enzo.  Height: 5'5, weight: 220, hair: black, eyes: grey.  Please repeat for confirmation."

The person identifying himself as Aunt Bea read back the description and added a gruff, "Good work.

"Your mission is now over.  Please return to position A and await next assignment."

"Roger." he replied. "12 out."

He turned a dial on the antique radio all the way to the left.  Its warm glow slowly faded.

Then he took out a modern cell phone instead.  The aesthetic difference between that and the old radio was extreme.  As were the difference between his clients.  

Henery, or 'agent 12' if you like, had been in the business far too long to keep all of his eggs in one basket.  

Perhaps that made him a traitor to his country.  Perhaps one day he'd swing from a noose because of it.  But until that day came, he resolved to store up such a deep mountain of cash that he could wallpaper Grand Central Station with c-notes.  But that wasn't being too greedy, now was it?

Anyway, they'd never catch him.  He was too fast.  Too clever.  Too good.

The phone rang a few times and somebody on the other end picked up.  

"Hello, Jungle Tours Limited, who's calling please?"

"Ape-Man, this is Fortune Cookie." he replied.  When would people get sick of these silly code names?  'Henery' was just fine.  And it's not like all the spy ops in the world didn't have a folder on him already.  It was all just a silly waste of time.  But it was a part of the game, so what could you do?

He recited the same information he'd given to the US government agent.  This time his mission wasn't canceled, however.

"Fortune Cookie, an extra 50g if you can tell me about a character called 'Fong'.  He's a crazy upstart who seems to be positioning the pieces for something big."

"100g" he replied.  It was another stupid ritual part of the game.

"Done."

"Well Ape-Man, that's an easy one.  I can tell you right now Fong has already wrangled control of the underworld in these parts and is leveraging that to branch into politics.  He's a crazy duck that won't stop until he owns it all, or causes World War III, or both.  Oh, and one more thing you should know.  Fong is Enzo's..."

That was as far as he got when the .45 caliber bullet slammed into his chest like a sledge hammer.  He fell backwards and might have lived to tell the tale even at that.  But it was not to be, as the rotten downhill side of the boat's cabin gave way under his weight.  He tumbled with the splintered wood across the sloped deck and into the frigid water.

At that icy temperature he couldn't stay under for long, and when he surfaced he was met with a hailstorm of point-blank gunfire that no one could've survived.


~8~
Say "cheese"

The week wore on.  Jane and Misty continued their friendly routine with window shoppers.  This was a dangerous game, however, and ultimately turned on them.  Yet it was a pleasant sort of backfire.  In acting the part of old friends, they soon became old friends.   

Jane still held back about her mysterious falling out with Ditchwater Kansas.  All she could say when questioned was, "I'm not ready to talk about it."  There was nothing else to be said on the subject after that.

Apart from that, however, she spilled the beans on everything about herself:  favorite songs, hopes and dreams, secret crushes, ...everything!

Misty, for her part, did the same.  She even shared the surprising fact that she held a black belt or equivalent in nine different martial arts!

"Combat multiplier", she'd once called it.

Jane was curious what kind of 'combat' her diminutive little friend would ever find herself in.  She asked, thinking it would be a funny jest.

Misty immediately retreated into her shell as if caught with her hand in a cookie jar.

"Oh, you never know..." she said after a strangely long hesitation.

Oh well.  If I'm allowed all my secrets, maybe Misty should least get one of her own.  Jane concluded.


One afternoon, they'd turned over their counter to Ted for the late lunch shift and headed for their usual table in the food court.  There was a mom with a toddler seated at it.

"That's the problem with kids these days!" said Jane in a bad imitation of a gruff old man's voice.

"No respect for tradition!" came Misty's even worse impersonation.

In fact, the entire food court was packed that day.  It wasn't close enough to any major holiday to be a photo-op with a costumed character.  The rumor mill that flowed between the employees of the different stores even turned up dry on this one.  

It must have been just the luck of the draw.  Whether that was good luck or bad remained unanswered.

The two toted their lunch bags through the packed hallway of the mall proper, looking for someplace -- anyplace -- to sit and eat.  Jane's keen eye spied two teens vacate a bench not fifteen feet away.  The two rushed to claim it before anybody else did.

Misty ate a whole wheat tortilla, cottage cheese, and baby carrots while Jane dined on a lukewarm cup o' noodles.  They sat silently eating and watching the shoppers pass by.  People of all kinds bustled to and fro and provided a good snapshot of humanity and its many idiosyncrasies.

"Look at that!" exclaimed Misty out of the blue.

"What?"  Misty's statement was seemingly random and Jane didn't have any context to know what she was supposed to be looking at.

"Looks like they got in the new Sony ZX-800!  I've got to see this!"

She stood and walked to a cell phone kiosk in the middle of the wide hallway.  Jane, as usual, was in tow.  She hadn't the foggiest idea what a ZX-whatever was.  It was presumably something amazing though.

Even though Jane was only a few steps behind her friend, the pimpled kid at the counter was already digging out the new model cell phone by the time she got there.  Misty jabbered on and on to him about the exciting new features.  By his increasingly shorter replies to her tech-talk she apparently knew more about the gadget than he did.

Jane didn't get much out of the techno-babble.  A camera:  That would've been something.  But this?  She was pretty un-savvy as far as things like these were concerned.  

To her, a phone was a simple thing with one job.  She punched in some numbers.  The other end rang.  What more was there?

Misty saw how lost she was and realized she'd better explain.  "I've been saving up forever waiting for this thing to come out!"

As if to prove it, she produced her old phone.  It wasn't all that old in terms of years but definitely well worn, being held together with tape and a Hello Kitty sticker.  There was little doubt that even the sticker was structurally necessary.

She turned back to the salesman and showed it to him too.  "Can you transfer my number from this?"

"Sure, we can swap SIM cards, no problem." he replied.

"Hey, Misty?"  Jane tugged at her sleeve.  She was thinking of a side deal.  "If you're buying that one, could you make me a good deal on your old phone?"

Jane owned a cell phone, but it was for her old area code back in her old life.  And there it remained, back in her parents' house, right where she'd left it, many blessed miles far away from here!  But that also meant that she was in need of a means of communication in this area code (whatever it was).  Even Misty's decrepit cell could still deliver that much.

"Um."  The wheels in Misty's head turned.  They stopped in a devilish little grin which she quickly concealed.  "S-Sure." she concluded.

"Go get our lunch bags, won't you?" she asked politely, explaining, "By the time I get the paperwork done we'll need to head back."

Friday wore on, with Misty acting mighty peculiar.  Jane thought that her friend had all the poker face of a hyena, but couldn't for the life of her figure out what it meant.

When sales hit their daily three o-clock lull, Misty pulled out her new phone, shiny and pink, and began fiddling with it.  Installing this and that, familiarizing herself with the gadget - she was completely engrossed in it.

That left Jane bored with no one to talk to.  Her eyes drooped at being forgotten, but she sighed in resignation.  Part of being a friend is letting the other person have their fun.  She took to straightening up a countertop earring display to pass the time.

"Hey Jane!  Say 'cheese'!" Misty announced from behind her.

The suddenness of it caught her off guard.  Jane whirled around just in time to see her friend standing beside her with the new phone at the end of an outstretched arm.  Misty hit a button and there was a noise indicating that she had just snapped a picture of the both of them.

"Oh no!  I made a face!" protested Jane.  She made a grab for the phone, but was way too slow.

"Ha ha!  Too late!  That's going as the wallpaper now." taunted Misty. 

Just then a customer walked up so Misty put the new phone down next to the register.

The eye of the hurricane soon passed as the Friday evening crowd began to besiege the mall in like it was the beach head at Normandy.

The afternoon and early evening passed by in a blur.  Before they knew it, they heard the steady mechanical rattle of the store's security gate descending.

Jane began to ring out the register for the day.  "Crazy day!" she observed.

"Uh huh." said Misty.  She was already halfway into her jacket.  She grabbed her keys and hustled around through the exit gap in the counter.   

Jane decided that Misty must have someplace she needed to be.  Strange that she hadn't mentioned it though.  The hurried Misty was already within a few paces of the exit by the time the tape finished chattering out of the register.

Upon hearing it stop, Jane looked down.  That's when she noticed something pink by the register.

"Yo, Misty!  You forgot your new phone!" Jane shouted after her friend.

Misty froze with one hand on the door.  "No, I didn't."  Her voice had a coy ring to it,  but she held up her new phone as proof of the statement.

Jane was seeing double.  "Well then, what's..."  All of a sudden it dawned on her as all the pieces snapped together.

"You rat!" she shouted.

Misty smiled and practically danced out the door.


~9~
Avoidance

Sunday evening.  

Jane drove up to the small guard shack in front of the Moast residence.  She felt none of the uncertainty that had nearly turned her away the first time.  

She was so very glad that she hadn't left that night!  She and Misty had since become such close friends and she never failed to praise God for her!  He alone knew how badly she needed a friend in these difficult days!

Truly, the earlier dread she felt about approaching the imposing mansion was washed away without a trace and thoroughly replaced with anticipation of seeing her friend and the rest of the gang once more.

Her car idled at the gate, ready.  She waited for the guard to open it.  All he did was shoot back a confused look in return.

"May I help you, miss?" he asked.

He was the same guard, but perhaps he was forgetting her face?

"Yes.  I'm Janet Jones.  I'm part of the Bible Study." she explained.
(Not, "I'm here for..."  Not, "I'm going to..."  But instead, "I'm part of...")

The man shook his head.  "I'm sorry miss. There's no Bible Study here."

Now Jane was confused.  "But I was here last week...  Misty... Ms McCready... that kid who likes pizza...  um...  'weird' Wendell!  That's it!  Are they here?" 

A blank stare is all she got in return.

Then she decided that there must be some kind of misunderstanding.  She tried another angle.

"Wait.  Are you saying, 'there's no Bible Study at all tonight', or that, 'it's just not happening here'?"

She caught a slight twitch of the man's mouth.  And there was also a hesitation before he re-delivered the earlier answer instead of addressing the question.  "I'm sorry but they are not here."

"The Moasts are they --"

He cut her off, delivering the same answer for a third time.  "I'm sorry."

This time, his tone had a certain finality to it.  He was resolute as a mule with all four legs braced against the ground.  This gate was just plain not going to be opening and that was that.

As Jane turned her car around, it briefly flickered across her mind that they were avoiding her - like some sort of unpopular kid in highschool.  She'd been that kid!  Old emotions of rejection began lumping up in the back of her throat.  Worse yet, she felt her tear ducts beginning to fill.

She stopped and adjusted the rearview mirror to get one last look at the stately house.  Thankfully, the driveway was devoid of cars.  Her self-doubt convinced her with certainty that everybody was there, laughing and having a grand old time without her.  Probably even joking and giggling about 'that loser' being turned away at the gate.

"But there are no cars." she tried to talk sense into herself.  There was nobody home.  Simple as that.

So why did this feel all wrong?


The next day at work Jane tried and completely failed to disguise the hurt in her voice.

She was already at the Blevens jewelry counter when Misty walked in.  Her alleged friend silently took up her position and began preparing things for the day.

'Alleged' friend.  Jane thought.  Her mind couldn't believe that was true either.

Jane could no longer keep a lid on what was weighing on her heart and jumped right into the middle of it.  "So, I guess I missed you guys last night, huh?"  It was meant to sound like casual, friendly conversation.  Jane wanted to kick herself for how obviously non-casual it sounded.

"Um, yeah." said Misty.  She didn't meet her friend's gaze.

"So where was everybody?"

"Oh, you know."

Of course she didn't know!  How would she know?  Why wouldn't Misty spill it?

"What's going on Misty?"

"Nuthin."

The terse answers were only feeding Jane's rising temper.  For her part, she was expending considerable effort to keep a lid on her emotions and not say something she'd regret.  Her dry throat lent a course edge to her voice though.

"Come off it.  Tell me the truth.  If you guys don't want to hang out with me, that's fine.  I can take it."  (Though that probably wasn't true.  She'd probably break down and cry and not come out of her shell for months.)

Misty looked up for the first time.  "Of course I want to hang out with you.  You're the best friend I ever had!"

Even in her present fit of rejection Jane's pessimism still couldn't get her to doubt it.

"But the others don't like me, do they?"

"You're being silly.  Of course they do."

"Then where were you guys last night?"

"I..."  She gritted her teeth.  "I... I...  I can't say."  Her face turned away.

All right, that's it!  Jane sucked in plenty of air and was about to lay down some serious ballyhoo when Mister Blevens walked past, making his rounds.

"Hey!  What's going on here?" he demanded.

It was a rhetorical question.

"You two stop havin' your hissy fit on company time.  Leave it for the parking lot.  Get ready to face those customers.  Come on, big smiles now!"  He flashed a shark-like grin that looked more terrifying than friendly.  There was a reason he usually ran the store from the back room.

Jane spent the rest of the day trying to force herself to be mad at her friend.

That ugly game made for a long day.  After what seemed like an eternity it did end.  The mall gate was closed and Jane immediately stepped out from behind the counter intending to give a cold shoulder and make for the door.  Before she could carry out her plan Misty caught her sleeve and looked up at her.

"Listen.  I know how it feels to be on the outside.  I've been on the outside my whole life too, remember?  Please understand that when I say 'I can't tell you' it means just that -- I can't tell you.  But..."  Strangely, she glanced around the room like she was a secret agent from some Hollywood action movie checking for any spies that might be watching.  Keeping with that image, she shifted into a whisper.  "But all I can tell you is:  Don't give up!"

Misty let go of the sleeve and made her own way towards the door.  Jane was left  standing at the counter.  Alone.  Alone, and frankly, quite baffled at the enigmatic advice.

"That was really weird Misty!" she called after her.

Misty only smiled warmly in reply.  It was the smile of a friend, but it was also dripping in concern.


~10~
Chasing Wendell

According to rumor, Giovanni's Pizzeria was the best pizza joint in town.  Their local renown came from their "Great Isle of Sicily" special.  If the ads were to be taken literally, a pizza the size of the Mediterranean island itself!

Jane was here because she'd done her homework.  That wasn't the only factoid she'd learned. 

She'd also learned that there were five different Wendell or W, Frank, St Frank or St Franks in the phone book.  Only one of these persons answered to phone or doorbell.  But, it just-so-happened that all of the four remaining ones lived within a two mile radius of this particular pizzeria.

It also just-so-happened that the greasy, meat-laden, Sicily special went on sale for half price on Wednesdays.

In other words, all stars were in line for the pizza-loving 'weird' Wendell to make an appearance this very evening.

That makes sense?  Right?   Jane sure hoped so.  Otherwise, sitting till closing time crouched in the shrubbery of the strip mall might've seemed a little crazy.

Okay, it was crazy anyway.  She'd be lucky if nobody spotted her and called the cops, let alone tracking down the man of the hour.  And that was only if Misty's cryptic, "Don't give up." advice somehow meant... something like this.

That was a lot of assumptions.

She was contemplating calling the whole thing off when a small hatchback pulled into a spot near the pizzeria's entrance.  What parts of the body that hadn't rusted and fallen off were a chalky, faded green color.

Its equally faded bumper sticker bore a broadly grinning cartoon badger.  "I Brake for Tasty Flakes (tm)", he proclaimed proudly.  The sticker had evidently come from a breakfast cereal box of some kind.  Though which decade it had originated from was a matter up for debate.

Jane racked her mind.  Was that one of the cars from the Moast driveway the night of the Bible Study?  

It could be.  It seemed familiar enough.

The question answered itself when the long, spindly legs of Wendell St Frank unfolded themselves from the driver's side door.  Sneakered feet hit the pavement.  Slowly, like an insect emerging from its tiny green cocoon, the rest of his body pulled itself out after them.

He was already entering the pizzeria's front door open when Jane climbed out of the bush.  She flicked a few leaves from her hair and trotted towards the entrance.

When she was still four paces from the door the air was already thick with the telltale scent of basil and bread.  Anybody dropped blindfolded on that sidewalk could easily identify what type of shop it was.

The smell grew stronger still when Jane pulled the door open.  Now it was mixed with a warm and heady steam.  The motion of the door caused small a bell suspended from a string to let off a soft tinkle noise.  A hostess looked up from her podium at the sound of it.

"Yes, miss?  How many will it be?" she asked politely.

"That tall guy who just walked in..."

"He's over there at the take-out counter."  She gestured in the direction.

Jane thanked her and walked quickly off in the way indicated.

Wendell had already handed his check to the man at the counter and was just picking up a piping-hot (and disturbingly huge) pizza box.  It looked like it could double as its own table if it had legs.

Grease steadily soaked through the bottom in a widening dark smudge.  Jane's stomach felt a little sick at the mere concept of eating that much grease and cheese in one sitting.

"Hiya Wendell!" she called in her friendliest voice.  She waved as well.  She was laying on the 'old buddy, old pal' routine pretty thick.

He turned around.  Recognition crossed his face.  And then he said, "Nope.  Sorry Jane.  You must have me confused with somebody else."

The lie, while patently ridiculous to her, seemed to have made the pizza man concerned.  He tapped (the alleged) Wendell on the shoulder.  He waved his hand about wildly, gesturing often back at the personal check with Wendell's name on it.  His words were half in Italian and half in English, but 100% full of concern over whether he was about to be stiffed the price of one large Sicilian.

Wendell gave him a wink and a thumbs-up gesture in reply.  This calmed him a little bit.  Not a lot; just a bit.  Whether or not he'd let the kid leave though...

Jane continued.  "Aw, come on, friend.  Why don't we go have a talk.  Eat a little pizza... have a little chat... what 'd ya say?"

He didn't know what to say.  So he asked a question instead.  One that they both knew the answer to.  "What do you wanna talk about?"

"Oh, you know... the weather... sports... movies... where the Bible Study is meeting?"

By all outward appearances Wendell was long and lanky; all elbows and kneecaps.  Maybe that's why Jane didn't notice his hand's lightning-quick move until it had already happened.

She never even saw him wing the grenade, but did see it slide across the tile floor, rebound back a couple of feet off the base of a nearby booth, and then go off.

Instead of an explosive, it was a gas grenade - a fast-acting thing too!  The room was almost instantly full of a choking white smoke.  It wasn't toxic.  Just overwhelming.  It smelled like a delightful combination of burnt marshmallows and wet dog.

The fire alarms, being simple-minded little devices, all noticed the smoke at once and at once began to wail away.  The automatic sprinklers kicked on as well.  Amid the cacophony of clanging bells and shouting customers, Jane also heard, barely, a faint tinkle -- the front door bell!

Her senses served her well and she found her way directly to it without bumping into anything.  She couldn't say as much for whatever chaos was going on in the kitchen as a stack of dishes was heard crashing to the ground.

Out of kindness to the others inside, she kicked down the leg of the door stop so that the smoke could clear a little.  She felt a great deal of pity for the restaurant owner.  This mess would take weeks to get cleaned up!

She ran towards to the green car where Wendell's pizza rested on the roof and where he fumbled with his keys.

"Wendell!  Wait!" she called.

Fire engines were heard and he swung his head around just in time to see them pulling into the parking lot.  It was the only exit and there would be no getting out that way now.  He jammed the keys into is pocket, grabbed his pizza, and ran.

Oh no you don't... She followed in hot pursuit.  A new determination was welling up within her.

Wendell came to a clean, but narrow, alley between two of the strip mall buildings.  He paused just long enough to respond to her demand with a challenge.  "You've gotta catch me first!"  He ducked into it and was off like a shot with Jane chasing hard after him.

When he reached its end, she saw him dart to the left.  When her time came, Jane did the same.  

He vaulted a barb-wire fence strung between the rear access lane of the strip mall and the farmer's field it abutted.  The field was newly plowed under and all bare dirt.  That meant there were no plants to hinder his movement so he broke across it at top speed.  He'd evidently raced track and field at some time in his past.

The barb-wire would've hung up most people, but a good Kansas farm girl like Jane could take a fence with the best of 'em.

Wendell's longer legs served him well as he dashed across the vacant field, but he was encumbered by the aerodynamics of the pizza box.  Also, Jane had a secret weapon of her own:  sheer pig-headed determination.  Because of these two advantages, she slowly gained on him, inch by inch.

Given a long enough stretch she would've caught up to him eventually.  'Eventually' never came as he straightaway abruptly ran out before she could close the gap.

The field ended with another fence, a dead-end road, and a construction site surrounded by chain link.  Wendell vaulted, crossed, and climbed.  So did Jane, but she wasn't as agile and much of what distance she'd gained on him was lost.

The fence had plastic privacy slats interwoven with the links which not only concealed everything beyond it, but also made it even harder to climb.  But climb, she did.

As she dropped to the ground on the other side she glanced left and right.  Which way had he gone?  She hadn't been able to see.

Jane allowed herself a moment or two to catch her breath.  She heard the telltale clank of feet on pipe.  It came from the construction scaffold directly in front of her.  Following the noise, she looked up and saw him climbing.

He shot up it like a monkey, but Jane had the advantage of having both arms free while he still stubbornly balanced the Sicilian with one hand as he climbed.  Wendell looked like some kind of Donkey Kong pizza delivery boy.

At nearly fifty feet in the sky he stopped at a level and darted along the weathered, paint-stained planks of the scaffold.

Jane reached it and started to run in pursuit, but immediately stopped herself again.

At the end of the scaffold he had stopped running.  Instead, he faced her.  And he was smiling.  

Three paces beyond him was nothing else but sky, and eventually, ground.

"Nice try." he grinned, taking a bite of the pizza slice in his hand.  "Real nice, actually.  You almost caught me."

Jane mistakenly took this as another silly dodge like the one he'd given back at the pizza parlor.  That was the thing about Wendell.  He was just ridiculous enough to make you not know when he was being serious.

He stuffed the pizza slice in his mouth and held it clenched in his teeth like a dog bone.
Then he spun, took the last three steps at a run, and jumped with all his might.

Jane stepped forward involuntarily and reached out.
