As Wendell leapt, his fingers snagged an aluminum cross-bar dangling from the overhanging level of scaffold.  A soft zip noise was heard like fabric being dragged across a surface and Wendell was at once soaring through the blue sky.

On a hang-glider!

Jane gawked in amazement, her mouth making a face like a goldfish blowing bubbles.  

Her breathing slowly returned to a normal rate as she watched the glider shrink away into the horizon.

This whole thing felt like a set-up.

She turned to leave, but looked down.  There, lying at her feet, was the hard-won pizza.  He must have dropped it!  Jane may have lost the contest but took a moment's satisfaction that she had at least won a conciliation prize.  

Even that changed when she opened the lid.  There, spelled out in pepperoni, were the two words, "GOOD JOB".  

A peace offering, perhaps?

She looked out at the hang-glider one last time.  It was now little more than a speck in the sky.  'Weird Wendell', indeed!


Jane took her time climbing back down from the scaffold, more slowly and carefully than she had come up.  As she did so it gave her mind a chance to process some of the data her senses were flooding her with.

For starters, this was a huge scaffold.  Tall enough for a six storey building.  And yet this was not a business district, but empty farmland.  There were no other tall buildings around.  Come to think of it, there was no building here even!  There was no framework.  No foundation.  Not even a hole.  Just scaffold.

What purpose it had was puzzling and impossible to tell.  For all intents, it could have been built here for nothing more than exactly what she'd just seen it used for -- a hang-glider launchpad.

Once she got to the ground, Jane re-traversed the chain-link fence (which was even more difficult with a pizza than Wendell made it look).  She noticed that the fence had a tin construction sign fastened to it with crimped wires.  It read, "Coming soon: Bowling Alley", and then in smaller text, "Moast Construction Services, Inc."

She smiled.  The world's first six storey bowling alley?  
Doubtful.  

And seeing the Moasts' name associated with it?  
That didn't make it make sense, but it did explain why it didn't make sense.  There was certainly much more to that couple than what met the eye.

By the time Jane finally returned to the pizzeria parking lot, she had already developed a certain expectation as to what she'd find.  It was met in full.  Wendell's small green car was gone -- silently and efficiently removed by parties unknown.

"Don't give up." Misty had said.

This is going to be trickier than I thought.


~11~
Air Siberia

Somewhere deep within the icy Siberian tundra a relic of a plane ground to a halt on the remote gravel runway.

The aircraft was some kind of cast-off World War II short-range bomber that had been gutted and had wooden benches bolted to the floor in place of the armament.  The emblem on the side of it bore the image of a slender brown snake and said something indecipherable in faded Russian.  That's what passed for a commercial flight in this section of the world.

A pair of air-traffic controllers watched from a log and tin air tower.  They saw its door swing open and two men step out.  They were hard to make out from this distance but seemed American.  They reached the bottom of the steps and began to run to get out of the stinging wind.

The man in the rear stepped on a football-sized cobblestone at the edge of the runway.  The rock went one way, his body went the other, and his ankle decided to do both.

His companion and a nearby airport security officer both ran to his side.  After looking him over the security man un-clipped the walkie-talkie from his belt and brought it to his lips.

The two men watching the event from the tower spun around as their radio set faithfully delivered what the man on the ground was saying.

"Officer 52 to tower...  Officer 52 to tower..."

"Go ahead 52."

"This guy hurt himself real bad.  Please dispatch an ambulance."

"Acknowledged."  He turned to his comrade and said with a scoff,  "Americans."

The other only shook his head and chuckled softly.


~12~
Winds of Indecision

After depositing Kurt at the local hospital for repairs, Jack wandered the streets of town, trying to get his head around things.

This part of town consisted of two or three large brick factories, surrounded by the workers' homes.

Jack found himself straying farther and farther into their midst.

The houses on either side of the lane were small but sturdy, painted in mostly blues and greens.  Few shared the same shape or design like you'd see in an American suburb.  Yet one commonality they all shared was a corrugated metal roof as a slippery defense against snow buildup.

It turned out Siberia wasn't the all-white ice slick one would normally imagine -- not this far South, anyway.  Though it was bitter cold when the wind whipped up, the only snow lie in piles along the North side of the houses, playing hide-and-seek with the sun.

Jack paused and looked at a heap of it.

I must be out of my mind.  He thought.  How can I do it?

Jack had been out on secret assignments before.  He and the others were tested and proven in their ability to handle any number of dangers involved.

The problem is, that was when he was with a team.  Now, with Kurt out of commission, he was a team of one.

He should scrub the mission.  He really should.  That would make the most sense.

In fact, if he didn't turn around and head home right now, he'd be in big trouble with Michael -- if he even made it out alive!

It wasn't a matter of pride, or proving that he had the mettle to do it himself.
(Or, was it?)

Still, there was something about this particular mission that he couldn't quite shake.

A pair of children in fur-trimmed jackets laughed and chased a soccer ball as it bounded down the pock-marked street.

It made him smile a little.

If this thing is for real, he thought, I can't just walk out on these people.

At that moment the sun slid behind a patch of clouds.  The chill was instantaneous.

Jack subconsciously looked to see where it went.  In fact, he didn't realize he had done so until he felt something collide with his ankle.

Pnut' myach! called older of the kids.

Da!  Da!  Pnieto! urged the other.

Jack looked down at the ball by his feet.  Of course he couldn't turn back!  Not now!

He hooked it with the top of his foot and sent it sailing over the kids' head.

With a squeal of delight they took off after it.

Jack took one last look over his shoulder in the direction of the airstrip -- the way back home.  Then, after a deep breath for strength, ventured ahead alone.


~13~
Chasing McCready

Jane brought Wendell's pizza home and, being quite poor at the moment, decided to eat it.

Big mistake!

The grease did an efficient job of taking her out of commission through the night and most of the next morning.  She was barely able to get to work and afterwards went straight home to bed.  

The entire day had to be written off, but did give her a chance to read her Bible and pray.  And that much was never a waste of time.

It was a fleeting thought, but Jane wondered, Maybe some of the circumstances that trip us up are designed by the Lord to slow us down enough to pay attention for a change.  

Or maybe it's just me.  Hard to say.

Either way, she used the time of convalescence to good advantage.  She prayed about the group and about Wendell, and indeed about a great many other things which remain private between her and the Lord.

Getting at Wendell by following the food was a no-brainer.  She'd almost wondered if she could have caught him with a trail of bread crumbs and a box propped up with a twig.

Anyway, the strategy was almost successful.  Therefore she decided the plan might be valid enough to continue with that same line of reasoning.

To boil it all down:
First:  What did she know about each person from the Bible Study?
And second:  How could she use that information to anticipate where they were going to be at any given time?

Let's say she wanted to track down Ms McCready next.  What did she know about her?

She's a sweet old lady with an Irish accent.  -- Not much there.

Oh!  But she also loves to bake.  What about that?


Jane found herself pushing a cart up and down the aisle of the little neighborhood grocery store.  

Her feet ached and she'd been there long enough that the stock boys were beginning to wonder why she hadn't finished up her shopping and gone home yet.  Two had approached her, thinking she was unable to find something she was looking for, while a third started smiling and winking every time she walked past.  

Jane rolled her eyes.  She really hoped she'd see Ms McCready before the pimple-faced kid asked her to the junior prom or something.  

Jane shuddered.  Thank you, brain, for that thought.

To top it off, the game she now played was a far, far longer shot than the one with Wendell, and she knew it full-well.

There were only two possible McCready's in the phone book and they each lived on opposite ends of town near completely different markets.  Furthermore, that was making the rash assumption that a single woman like her would even have her name listed.

So it all came down to a flip of the coin and to luck.

No, that was probably a bad term.  Call it 'Divine Providence' instead.

Jane circled around from the freezer section to the produce aisle one more time.  Another wink.  And another, why me?

Despite her coming and going conviction that her quest held the blessing and approval of the Lord Almighty, she was stunned to look up and see Ms McCready herself bending down to get something off a low shelf.

"Hiya Ms McCready." She tried the same opening line she had used on Wendell.  If Ms McCready ran though, Jane felt a lot more confident about her ability to catch her.

"Hello dearie!  Glad I caught ye!"

Jane's step faltered.  She was a little confused about the second half of that statement.  Ms McCready seemed to be confused about who was the searcher and who was the searchee.  Odd, but Jane shook it out of her head.  It probably meant nothing.

Ms McCready continued, "Could ya please get those tomatoes on the bottom shelf.  Me old knees ain't what they used t' be."

Jane knelt and handed up several good looking ones until Ms McCready called a halt.

She stood.  Ms McCready pointed to another stand.

"Since you're here, would ya mind addin' that big bag o' apples over there?"

They made their way to the register where Ms McCready paid for her purchases.

Jane followed the woman to her car.  It was the red Ferrari from the night of the Bible Study!  Jane had assumed that that car was a part of the Moasts' collection.  It seemed strangely out-of-place for the matronly old Irishwoman.

Ms McCready slid the single paper bag of tomatoes into a small gap behind the seat and then apologized that the apples would have to ride on Jane's lap.  Somewhere in the process she neglected to specify where she was planning on going, and also glossed over the little detail of asking if Jane was coming along.  It was all simply taken for granted.

After spending all morning going in circles with a shopping cart, Jane certainly wasn't about to argue the point.


The bright red Ferrari zipped easily down the county road.  Its top was down allowing the wind to flow through the ladies' hair.  It was an amazing vehicle.  And Jane said so.

Ms McCready patted the dash in response like it was a good, obedient pet.  "Yea, she's a beaut."

They drove in silence for another mile before McCready broke it.  "So, you're a single and a 'photography enthusiast' as 't were?"

"Yep, cameras are a lot safer than boys for me.  Especially these days.  I got a lot of personal baggage to deal with before I even think about something like that again."

It was strange how the simple and kindly voice of the Irishwoman had a way of dragging information out of you; information that you wouldn't have given out on pain of death.

"So how about you?  Is there some handsome beau in your life?" Jane asked Ms McCready.

"Ah, no dear.  I've had two husbands, and none more for me, thanks!  Cars are m' love now."

Jane was prepared to leave it at that, but Ms McCready continued, "Ye see, the trouble with men is they get punctures too easy."

Jane wasn't sure whether she wanted to know any more than that.  It sounded iffy.  But curiosity got the better of her.  " 'Punctures'?"

"Aye.  When your car gets a puncture, ye call the Triple-A.  When your husband gets a puncture, all the red stuff comes out and ye find yourself sad and alone.

"No thank ye very much!  It's cars for me now.  Cars are m' love and m' excitement!"  As if to accentuate the disturbing picture, she put more foot to the throttle and a shudder of raw power shook through the fiberglass monster.
 


The car skidded sideways to a halt at a gravel dead end.  Ms McCready unbuckled and slowly got out.  Jane did the same.  If nowhere had a middle, this would be it.  It looked like the only purpose of this location was as a spot for very lost people to turn around.

Besides the lonely road, there was a badly leaning barbed-wire fence, a decrepit and rusting old John Deere tractor, and untold miles of some crop, new and green, sprouting from the ground.

Ms McCready told Jane to open the bag of apples.

While Jane struggled with the adhesive plastic tie, Ms McCready put on a pair of what looked like shop class safety goggles and a pair of earmuffs.

"PULL!" she shouted out of the blue.

Jane jumped visibly.  "W-wha?"

"No, dear.  The apples.  When I says 'pull', you throw one up, high as y' please.  Got it?

"Now then...

"PULL!"

Jane threw the apple.  McCready drew her sidearm and blasted it to kingdom come.  She gave a hearty laugh of approval at the violence.  

Many would call it a "gun" or a "pistol".  An expert would call it a "Smith & Wesson .308 Special".  Ms McCready just called it her "wee glove box gun".

"These days I really don't use guns much anymore.  Too noisy.  But..."

"PULL!"

Another apple.  Another direct hit.

"...but a little target practice never hurt anythin', right?"

Jane nodded.  Sure.  Why not?  She didn't understand what was happening in the slightest.

Each shot sent a ripple through McCready's ample cellulite, but beyond that she held herself solid as an oak.  A dozen or two more apples went to the great hereafter before she saw fit to speak again.  (To a person keeping score, she never missed a one.)

"Ever shoot?" asked Ms McCready, finally.  As she did so, she kicked open the revolver, squinted down inside, and began reloading yet again.

Jane nodded.  "A little.  With a rifle back home.  Papa always..."  Then she changed her wording.  "My father always said we never knew when we'd have to defend our lives or our land."

"Very wise... Very wise..." the older lady approved, snapping the gun shut with a deft flick of the wrist.

Jane didn't know what to say to that.

She doubly didn't know what to say when McCready made as if she wanted to hand her the pistol.  

Jane shook her head as a "no thanks", but the other lady offered it again with more insistent, almost pleading, gesture.

Jane took it and looked the gun over carefully.  When as comfortable as she'd ever be, she offered up a meek, "Pull?"

Ms McCready pitched an apple high into the sky.  Jane popped off two quick shots and then a third just before it hit the ground with a thwock and bounced.  The first two missed entirely, where the third accidentally punched through a parked tractor's tire.

Air began to hiss out.

Ms McCready chuckled and gestured for her gun back.  Jane gave it most gladly.

"Nice shootin' there, Tex." she teased.  The phrase was such a bad Irish-twanged rendition of John Wayne that you'd really have be there to appreciate it.  Ms McCready laughed so hard at her own joke that she was standing doubled over, with her hands on her knees.

Jane couldn't help but wonder at that.  'Help me with the tomatoes.', and 'I'm too old.', she'd said back at the market.  But then there was the fast sports car, and the joking, and moving, and leaping around, and shooting guns with ease...  She'd even chucked that apple every bit as high as Jane could, if not higher.  Jane couldn't help but think it all added up to one thing.  There was a big act going here with the, 'tired old woman who loves to bake', routine.

Yet if it was all for show, was McCready trying to convince others or herself that that's who she was?

Once the humor wore off McCready finally holstered the firearm, still smiling.  She waved the tips of her fingers at the now deflated tractor tire.  "Well, I think we'll just count that 'un as a hit if anybody asks."  She hopped spryly up on the hood of the car and patted the spot next to her for Jane to sit.  From nowhere she produced two bottles of soda and handed Jane one.

"So... is this some kind of test, or something?" asked Jane, finally giving voice to her suspicions.

"Ach!  Am I that obvious?  Never could get the hang o' that bit.  I'm tragically non-sneaky, y' see...  Always came back t' bite me back in me days with the SEALS."

"You were a Navy SEAL?" Jane asked, surprised.

"Mmm." sufficed for a time since her mouth was filled with drink.  She swallowed it and continued.  "More 'with' than 'in'.  Y' see our little team did things that..."  

She rubbed her chin, considering how to put into words all the images that came flooding back into her mind.

"Let's just say 'things'... of the 'unofficial' kind... an' leave it at that." 

Jane raised an eyebrow.

"Bah!" she waved away the memories.  "That was a long, long time ago..."

Ms McCready turned the conversation back around to Jane instead.  "But wha' about you though?  You got the same disease as me when it comes t' hidin'."

Jane gave her an, 'I don't know what you're talking about', look.

McCready gestured at the overall expression with her bottle.  "See? 'Tis exactly what I'm talkin' about, right there."  And then she cut to the chase.  "Had a fallin' out with yer folks did ye?"

Jane was cornered now.  She could only nod.

Thankfully, Ms McCready wasn't one to pry it out of her.  If she tried, Jane would've resisted like a stone statue.  Since she hadn't tried though, part of Jane suddenly wanted to tell her everything.

Maybe another time... Jane thought.

Ms McCready decided to confess her own story instead.  

"Never had that 'un mesself.  M' family was always close.  Immigrated when I's only six, so bein' in a new country, ya kinda have t' be close.

"Nah.  'Corn whiskey', that's the name 'o my daemon."  She eyed her soda with a twinge of disappointment in her eye.  It wasn't what she wanted to be drinking, but, "That stuff'll take you right down the garden path and straight up the ramp t' the slaughterhouse, it will!"

"Ever drink?" she added.

Jane thought back to her wilder days.  She thought back to a certain Friday night in the woods.  About a red Ford and a tailgate.  And about a boy who thought a couple cheap beers would buy what he wanted from her.  It didn't work (thankfully!) but she'd walked home to a stiff lecture and he'd driven home with a red handprint across his face.

All this she told to Ms McCready, who, strangely, was not shocked in the least.  Jane didn't know why she'd said it, nor why the lady took it in stride.  Then again, Ms McCready must've seen more than her share of trouble over the years. 

"Well."  Ms McCready looked at her wrist as if there was a watch on it.  "Time t' be headin' back.  Thanks fer spendin' time with an old has-been like mesself."

She considered herself a "has-been"?  The applesauce all over the hood of the car declared otherwise.


~14~
Police Station

The police commandant sat behind a cold metal desk.  The desk stood beside a pair of cold metal file cabinets opposite the cold metal door.  Everything about the room was cold.  Even the lone incandescent bulb did nothing to alleviate the chill of it.  

Perhaps that's why the man wore his jacket and fur-trimmed hat even indoors.  His thick mustache was also chosen for its thermal benefit rather than as a fashion preference.

Igor Rurick was a respected inspector in a land of many inspectors, but nearly no respected ones.  For twenty years he'd sat behind this desk, too competent to fire and yet too honest to promote.

His finger stroked his nose from the bridge to the tip.  It did it again.  And again.  It was his strange habit of doing when he was lost in thought.

The typewritten report that lay spread out on his desk had him utterly confounded.  He had seen one or two genuine peasant uprisings before and also many manufactured ones.  This one looked like neither.  

But what was it then?

There was a light tap at the door.

"Dah?"

The door opened.  An old babushka woman all in black popped her head around it.  "Vosta Novitch." she greeted, and then pointed at the nozzle of a tired old rolling vacuum cleaner which trailed behind her.

She might as well.  Igor was getting nowhere.  He swept the papers all together into their file folder and tucked it under his arm.

He doffed his hat slightly at the hunched cleaning woman as he passed.  Maybe a change of scenery would make this make sense.

The woman shuffled into the room and turned on the vacuum.  She was a strange old bat.  She vacuumed the same spot over and over as if she didn't expect the room to get any cleaner.  

She didn't show her face much but when she did it bore a strange resemblance to a woman with a pram and a map of Oklahoma City.  A woman who was no woman at all, but a mysterious character named Jack.

As the cleaning went on, she suddenly stood erect.  Her eyes were no longer ancient but young and vital.  Her form was no longer bent and doddering, but strong with broad shoulders.  It was almost like in the blink of an eye the old woman magically transformed into a young man, like a shape-shifting sorcerer from some fairy tale.

He walked straight for the filing cabinet and yanked out a metal drawer.  It grated loudly, but he'd anticipated that.  That's why the vacuum was still running.

His fingers brushed over the tabs of the file folders.  His quick eye deciphered each heading written in the strange Cyrillic characters of the Russian language.

Nothing.

He slammed it closed and went for the next drawer.  His finger ran along the tabs once more and then stopped abruptly.

"Missing!" he said aloud.

"You know, for an agent you're not very observant." came the unexpected reply in English.

Jack spun around to see Igor Rurick leaning against the door jamb.  His arms were folded casually, but there was an automatic 9mm pistol in his right hand.

He continued.  "If you weren't trying so hard to avoid eye contact you might have noticed that I took it with me.  Also, using the noise of the vacuum to drown out your searching was a good idea in principle, but it also made it so I could easily sneak up on you."

"Anything else?"  There was a note of hurt pride in Jack's voice.

"Yes." he replied, "Your Russian is passable, but you obviously learned it from a Muscovite.  We are four thousand miles from Moscow where the accent is completely different.  Would you expect somebody from Boston to stand out in Texas?"

"But I might have been from Moscow." Jack persisted.

"No.  People don't travel in Russia the same way they do in your country.  And when they do, they certainly don't travel to Siberia of all places.  No, the only people traveling out this far from civilization are either government officials or prisoners in their custody.

"And one more critique:  I caught that slight movement of your hip and the bend of your knee while I was talking just now.  Not only can I guess that you are right-handed but also that you are planning on making a dash for that window.

"I should advise against it.  Again, your observation skills are lacking.  There are bars on it."

Jack relaxed the muscles he had tensed for the sprint.  This man had him all figured out and there was no denying it.

"So what now?  You shoot me?  Send me to the gulag forever?  What?"

In reply he took a step forward and laid his gun on the desk.  He reached into his jacket and pulled out the missing folder instead.  This, he handed across to Jack.

" 'Now'?  Now we trade information."

Jack took the folder and looked down at it.  It was an option far preferable to what he'd been expecting.  "I have to tell you up front that we don't know much though."

"Nor do we." said Igor.  "If we did, the KGB would simply put a stop to it and take all the credit.  Instead they throw it in my lap to dodge whatever blame will come of it."

Jack flipped open the folder and translated.  He spoke as he read.  "There's a group of rebels... escaped cons, belligerent Bolsheviks, national traitors... all amassing an army somewhere near the steppes."

Jack tapped a photo that he came upon.  "This guy's their leader.  Goes by the assumed name Anton Ravenclaw.  We don't know his real name."

"Neither do we.  Please continue."

"We do know that he's calling himself a Czar like he plans on taking Russia back into imperial rule.  With himself as the emperor, of course."

Jack fanned out the few pages on the desk and photographed them one by one. 

"What else do you know?" probed the commander.

Jack shrugged.  He put away his camera and slid the papers back into their folder.  "Not much besides the precise GPS coordinates where this is all going down. That's the reason why I came all the way out here."

"Really?  Where is it, then?"

Jack smiled.  Igor was interested.  "Such a boring topic of conversation.  I'd rather go back to what you said earlier about bars on the windows, not being able to leave, that sort of thing..."

The commander's face tightened into a smile.  "I believe that's the first smart thing I've seen you do since we met.

"You're obviously not KGB or CIA but some independent agent.  That, combined with your offer to tell me the location, means that you have no interest in supporting the insurrection.  That places you on my side.

"For your part, the fact that you are even in my office means that you must have done your research on me as well and know me to be a stupidly honorable man.  Therefore, you can believe what I say.  And what I say is that in exchange for the coordinates you will go free."

He was right.  Everything about his record showed a man of integrity.

Jack grabbed a pen off the commandant's desk.  He wrote a series of numbers on the inside cover of the folder and closed it.

He handed it to the man on his way out.


~15~
Chasing Michael

Michael Moast was surrounded by untracked jungle, hot and sticky.  He could smell the plants both alive and growing and fallen and decaying -- the scent of life and of death locked in unrelenting combat.  Off in the distance, over the clatter of numerous chattering birds and monkeys, he heard an elephant bugle.

Sweat ran down his face and plastered his hair to his scalp and stung his eyes.

Slowly he raised both hands over his head and overlapped his thumbs as if making the universal sign for 'butterfly' in the sky.

The jungle was two things at once.  It was equal parts Congo and the inside of a building.  Equal parts sun filtering through the leaves and cold fluorescent fixtures.

Something roared.  A lion?  A passing truck?

He ran at the top of his speed past foliage whipping by and motivational posters.

He ducked, sprang, and flipped in a perfect forward hand-spring.

The landing was a short bounce.  Then, at just the right moment, his powerful muscles twanged like rubber bands as he leapt for a second time.

He sailed through the air and caught the tree limb and swung himself around and over the top of it.  It was also one of a set of Olympic uneven bars so he swung around it once... twice... and when he reached the top of the next swing he let go, twisted in midair, and caught the second bar.

Another swing.  This time when he was upside down, he stopped himself in that position.  There he held himself for a moment.  He took one or two slow breaths and blew them fully back out.

One... two... three... he began doing upside-down push-ups.  He reached 50.

Having finished his set, Michael simply released the grip of one hand and swung down by the fingertips of the other, gently depositing himself on the mat in one silky smooth movement.  Even that maneuver would've been a fete of strength for most people.  He performed it with a casual ease as if it were as everyday an act as flipping a pancake.

It actually was an everyday act.  Every day.  Right here.  The mat even showed a permanent divot at his landing spot to prove it.

Michael grabbed a folded towel from a courtesy stand, wiped his face, and wrapped it around his neck for later.  The sweat would be back soon of course.

To cool down, he headed for the weight benches.  He was a strong man to be sure, but weights were not really his bag.  He mostly used them to round out his regimen.  The knotted muscle and quick reflexes given by cardio, that was far more important to Michael than pumping iron to look like some big man on the beach.

For him it was about brute survival and 'law of the jungle', not about turning heads.

He headed straight for his favorite weight machine at the end of the row next to the treadmills.

He set it, lay on the bench, and began pumping iron.  The river of sweat returned with a vengeance.  Muscle crawled over muscle as his huge, sweat-glistened shoulders and pectorals drove the bar up, down, and back up again, time after time.

Michael hardly noticed the person who climbed onto the nearest treadmill, keyed in their desired settings, and started running.

"Do you come here often, Michael?" asked the person.  It sounded like a female voice.  He didn't ask how she'd learned his name.  Must've been some gabby attendant.  

That was the problem with these places.  A lot of single people used it as a meet-market.  Between them and the people who came to primp and show off, those simply there to get in shape were surprisingly rare.  But that was Michael.

"Please, Miss.  I'm trying to exercise." he dismissed her politely.

"I can see." she persisted.  "But I have something to ask you."

Either she wasn't taking the hint and a pick-up line would follow, or she must be some 'fast track' junior manager from one of his businesses that thought she was being clever by cornering him here.  Either way her proposition was doomed.

"I'm sure you do." he said dismissively.

"You don't recognize me, do you?" she asked.

He hadn't yet looked in that direction and with all the sweat in his eyes, couldn't have seen her if he did.  The voice tugged at his subconscious, and so he stopped his exercise, mopped his face with the towel, and turned to look.

It was Jane Jones.

"Oh. You." he said.  And then went back to his exercises.

No, "How did you find me?" no running away like Wendell, no white-knuckle car ride like Ms McCready, but simply, "Oh. You."?  What's that even supposed to mean, anyway?

Jane ran a few more miles while she was contemplating what to say next.  Michael was, himself, a man of few words, so this arrangement worked out absolutely fine for both.

"You don't think I can keep up with you, do you?" she finally said.  She was sure of that much, though still unaware of exactly what it meant.  There was also a note of challenge in her statement as well.  It held a tone of, "try me!" 

Michael responded simply, "No.  No I don't."

"How do you know?"

"I know."

"I ran down Wendell."  It was stretching the truth.

"I know."

"I out-shot Ms McCready." It was stretching the truth in ways that would send Gumby to the chiropractor.  "But I suppose you know that too."

"Yep."

Jane muttered under her breath, "... beat you too."

Michael nearly dropped the weight bar on himself he was laughing so hard.  It was an odd, deep chuckle.  Like a lion laughing at a funny joke.

He put the bar down and wiped his face again with the now saturated towel.  He took one look at it and chucked it like a basketball into a laundry bin across the way.

"You're on." he said.

Some part of Jane's brain was shouting - trying desperately to talk some sense into her.  "What in the world are you doing!??" it cried.  Jane really wished she'd have thought her little side comment through before saying it.

Now it was too late though.  Now her pride wouldn't let her take it back.  Not only that, but it would end all hope of breaking back into the strange group.

"How then?" asked Michael.

"What?"

"How would you like to try to beat me?"

"Oh."  The thought hadn't even crossed her mind.  So, she could pick any contest in the place she wanted?  Well that was something, at least.

She scanned counter-clockwise around the room:
Treadmill?  No.
Weights?  No.
Olympic gear?  No.  (She'd seen his stunts earlier!)
Step aerobics? No.
A boxing ring?  She shuddered. 
But there were some jump ropes.  Maybe jump ropes?  
Bikes?  No.
And she came right back to where she stood needing an answer.

Had she seen some hope in jump ropes?  When she was a little girl Jane was pretty good at double-dutch.  But, no.  Even with jump ropes this guy could easily eat her lunch.  She'd wind up in double-dutch in more ways than one.

Face facts,  she was going to be beat miserably no matter which one she chose and they both knew it.

But there he was... still waiting for an answer...

A sick thought crossed her mind but she hated it so badly that she instantly shoved it back down.

She was about to play the favorite and say "jump ropes" anyway, when the thought jumped back into her brain and hijacked control of her vocal chords.  "Boxing." she heard herself say.

Michael's eye's got wide.

Jane felt her cheeks and forehead getting hot.  This was going to hurt so bad!

"Are you... are you... sure about that?" he stammered.

At least she had the satisfaction upsetting the perpetual coolness of the rock-solid Michael Moast.  As far as the inevitable trip to the hospital? ...

"Bring it on." she completed the thought aloud.

They both walked to the ring.  Doubt followed Jane every step of the way there.  Now she was going to get beat up, sure as anything.  She hoped that at least bravery counted for something in Michael's book. 

The boxing ring held an odd and powerful aroma of vinyl and sweat socks.

Jane watched Michael select and lace up a pair of boxing gloves out of the corner of her eye.  She mimicked it.  When they were tied securely, she stood and turned to face him.  

She held both gloves close to her chin and tried to look like a tough character.  That was the beginning and the ending of her boxing training.  (It was learned from a pay-per-view boxing advertisement she'd seen on a bus stop once.)

Michael brought his gloves up similarly.  Well, at least that must be right.

She'd had plenty of fighting experience if you considered arguments.  If you counted only physical altercations, maybe a couple of hair-pulling hissy fits in junior high.  Michael's hair was trimmed short and the fingers on the boxing gloves weren't exactly movable so even that method of combat wasn't open to her.

Wait!  She knew something else about boxing!  You're supposed to bob and weave, right?

She started doing what she thought that might look like.

"What on Earth are you doing?" asked Michael, dropping his hands to his side.

"Bobbin' and weavin'."

"Oh." he said simply.

After watching her with a concerned expression for a few seconds, he spoke again.  "You know that boxing usually involves hitting the other person, right?"

Her face flushed.  She was angry at having her bluff called.  She was also angry at being patronized, and most of all, she was angry about being left out of the Bible Study!

She swung at Michael's face with all her might.

She never expected to hit anything.  

But she did!

He was stunned for a moment, not so much from force of the blow (it packed the full force of a piledriver from a butterfly), but from the fact that she'd actually done it.

She was about as stunned, but immediately went back to "bobbin' and weavin'".  Her confidence was much higher now.

Michael brought his guard up.
"You really don't know what you're doing, do you?"

"Of course!" she said confidently.  "I got you, didn't I?"

He hit her.  Square in the gut.

It was a pulled punch, but even so it felt like her spleen was about to come out her ears.  She doubled over.

"Do you know what you're doing?" he asked again more emphatically.

"Yes!"  Confidence was long gone but stubbornness remained.

He hit her in the head this time.  Again it was comparatively soft, and Michael wouldn't think of damaging the poor girl's face, but the konk on the side of the skull was plenty to send her sprawling into the ropes.  There she hung by an elbow.

"Do you?" he asked again.

She staggered dizzily back to her feet.  She was about to defy him again, but stumbled backwards.  

He stepped in and caught her arm with a glove to steady her.  With the other he lifted her chin to make eye contact.  His gaze leveled at her.  "Do you?"

She took a deep breath, just about said something defiant, but dropped her head and began to cry instead.

"No." she admitted between sobs.

She shrank to her knees and he let her.  She kept crying for a few seconds and then looked up.

"No, I don't know what I'm doing."  The sobs abated slightly but the tears still came. "Not a clue!

"It always seems like my life is so out of control -- so close that I can touch it, but it always manages to slip between my fingers.   The harder I try to keep it together, the more it all comes unraveled!

" 'Do I know what I'm doing',  Michael?  No!  Never!"

Jane hung her head again and just let the tears come.

Michael jerked laces of his gloves loose with his teeth and flung them into the corner of the ring.

He looked down at her.  "You lost." he said.  "Do you think that makes you a loser?"

Jane nodded.

He crouched down. "Let me let you in on a little secret.  I've been in over my head my whole life too."

She looked up, surprised.  Surprised, and wondering if he was just trying to be nice.  He wasn't.  He meant every syllable.

"In fact, I say if you're not in over your head, that's not life.  That's hiding from life.  And losing isn't what makes a person a loser.  Not trying is what makes a person a loser."

She didn't quite grasp it.  And he could tell.

"Listen Jane, I started out with nothing but the clothes on my back and poor grasp of the English language.  From that humble beginning I made a wealthy man of myself.  I married Melinda, a rich and beautiful heiress.  I bought her plenty of jewelry, a nice house, nice cars...  Everything was going great.

"And then I made a series of stupid mistakes that lost it all.  I can still see the terrified look on her face when they took the house.  

"For awhile I even thought I'd lose her too.  Poor old Melinda promised herself to me, 'for better or worse', but she didn't realize how literally I'd take her up on it.  (Perhaps no bride does.)

"But she stuck with me, and I worked my way back to the top.  Then, despite all my caution this time, the bottom dropped out of the market and I learned how to fail in a completely new way.

"Long story short, this is the third time I've been a self-made billionaire.  The most important thing I've learned through it all is that you win some, you lose some.  You can stay up nights worrying about it but trying to insulate yourself from failure is a fool's errand.  Life is trouble.  Try and avoid the trouble and all you avoid is the life.   

Jane looked up, "You mean it's actually good that I got beat up because at least it means I tried?"

He sat down cross-legged on the mat next to her.

"Sort of.  Let me put it another way.

"Many of the people I deal with in the business world act like the ends justify the means.  But it's actually just the opposite.  

"The ends come and go.  You can't control it.  Influence, sometimes, but never control.  If you think you can, you're only deluding yourself.

"No, it's the means that we have control over.  Better to faithfully live them out and accept whatever God sees fit to deliver in the end.  'Walk in faith and rely on Providence', that's my motto, anyway."

"So you're telling me I need to keep at it anyway, and not worry about whether I fail or not?"

He smiled and nodded.

They sat in silence a moment.  

At length Michael got up and headed for the mens' locker room.

"Wait." said Jane. "Where's the Bible Study?"

He looked around him in mock searching.  "Not here." is all he would say.

Michael stepped into the locker room but poked his head back out about 60 seconds later.

Jane stood at the end of the gymnastics mat with her hands stretched over her head.  She ran down the mat at full-tilt.  When she reached the end, she ducked, sprang, and landed in a heap.

He smiled.  It was a start.

Would he see her on Sunday night?  He sure hoped so.  


~16~
Ambassador

"Le Fleur!" shouted the Ambassador seated behind his mahogany desk.

"Le Fleur!  Where are you?"

A short, balding man fast-waddled into the room.  His coke-bottle glasses and rumpled suit added to the figure.  One might think by looking at him that he was a haggard, middle-man bureaucrat that could shuffle papers with the best of them, but had just about reached the end of his career advancement due to lack of charisma.  One would be right in thinking that.

"Le Fleur!  Give me that file!  Sapristi, sir!  Must I wait all day?  I am to meet the Russian in less than thirty minutes!  And there you are doddling like some..."

The shower of rants kept up their barrage until the man crossed the floor and placed Ambassador's coffee on his desk and the folder in his hands.  "Draco", said its label in bold, foreboding letters.

It was also stamped, "C.I.A. Top Secret" across the front.  Which, to the Ambassador, it might just as well have said, "Expensive".  And he really hoped it was worth the price he'd paid.

As far as political potential went, the Ambassador Rousseau was the polar opposite of Le Fleur.  He was tall and devilishly handsome.  Hit with the ladies.  Toast of the town.  And like any career politician with a bright future, totally incapable of thinking for himself.  No, everything from his opinions on nuclear proliferation to his choice of hair gel was a deliberate and calculated creation born of statistics.  And nothing more.

The Law was written by the finger of God and delivered by Moses.  The Ambassador's deepest convictions were written by pollsters and delivered by teleprompter.

That's how he came to be in charge of the Consulate.  Some nameless, faceless statistician somewhere had decided that getting this kind of credential would be ideal to fill out the "foreign relations" section of his resume and eventually help him maneuver all the way into the seat of Prime Minister.

No doubt on a usual day the avalanche of insults would've continued to shower down on poor Le Fleur, but today time was of the essence!  The Ambassador stood, put on his long wool coat, grabbed his travel mug, and made straight for the door.  

He carried enough political clout that he usually wouldn't have been in a hurry, even being this late.  People waited for him, not the other way around.  The receptionist out front noticed all this and realized that something abnormally big was afoot.

The Ambassador breezed by her, without leaving a single word behind to ease her curiosity.  Instead, he strode briskly for the elevators.  

He was in such a hurry that he hadn't even bothered to shut his office door behind him.  Le Fleur idly watched it swinging free on its hinges in the artificial breeze of the office air conditioning.

"Draco." mused Le Fleur.  His normally stone dead eyes seemed to have a strange new sparkle.


Mere moments later the Ambassador was down at the curb and climbing into the back seat of a taxi cab.  The diver was a tough and gruff looking man with Italian features whose accent suggested he'd washed over from Jersey with the garbage on a bad tide day.  It didn't matter much to the Ambassador as long as the guy could somehow get him through the Manhattan rush hour to his meeting.

"An extra one hundred U.S. dollars if you can make it in time!" he promised.  To the Ambassador, dropping more money solved any problem.

The driver acted properly shocked and impressed, but somehow it lacked real emotion.

Ambassador Rousseau nearly misplaced his travel mug while getting in.  Such an event would have been tragic, indeed as the only recourse would've been to resort to American coffee.  Heaven forbid!

The greasy cabbie noticed it on the roof and handed it back to him before shutting the door.  He seemed as concerned - if not more so - than the Ambassador about the near tragedy.

The Ambassador was doubly relieved to see that at least the filthy driver wore gloves so that his shiny cup wasn't marred with the man's greasy American fingerprints. 

The Frenchman took a sip of the coffee.  It seemed unusually bitter today.  But maybe it was just his attitude over the Draco matter flavoring the drink.

No matter.  He opened the file and looked over the dossier.  

"Enzo Fontana" was the name on the front sheet.  The words on the page seemed to describe a villain of the worst kind.  A killer through and through working for one of the worst crime syndicates in the world.  He had to nail this guy.  Between this and what the Russians knew, they could do it too, and possibly put an end to a global disaster the like of which had never been seen before.  (More importantly, it would do wonders for speeding his way up the political ladder.)

He took another sip.

Bitter, he thought again.

After several pages of stuff that made Vlad the Impaler seem like a pussycat in comparison, the Ambassador finally reached the last sheet.  An 8x10 surveillance photo of the maniac himself.

Though black and white and grainy as a pepper shaker the man was instantly recognizable.

"Hey!" shouted the Ambassador in horror.

A lump began to form in his throat and beads of sweat on his forehead as eyes glared back in the rearview mirror.  The same eyes as the photo!

The lump grew.  In fact, suddenly he couldn't breathe at all!  He wasn't hyperventilating, but still his lungs refused to fill!  Then there was the sharp, lancing pain that stabbed through his chest!

Gradually the cab pulled to the curb, stopped, and the cabbie turned around.  Besides that, he didn't move a muscle.  He only sat watching calmly.

The Ambassador's hot, sweaty, skin transformed into a thousand pinpricks of pain as his fingers groped and scratched futilely at the door latch.  And then at the windows.  And seats.  Anything that would make the pain stop!  He writhed on the bench seat shouting in anguish.  But no noise came out.  His body convulsed.  And then he sputtered and choked his last.

"Interesting." said the cabbie, jotting something down in a small spiral notebook.  Then he made a mental note as well.  He'd have to remember to shake hands and thank the new Ambassador Le Fleur for the tipoff the next time they met.

Then he drove off to find someplace to dispose of the body.


~17~
Chasing Melinda

Sunday eventually arrived once more and time was running out fast.  Of the two remaining members of the group, Melinda Moast proved to be a slippery character, without any pattern to her that Jane could determine.  

Dr Puttery was even worse.  He was just plain nowhere on the map.  Whatever secret hole he hid in, he apparently never left it.  Despite her best efforts, she'd found zilch on the man.  He was like a ghost or a legendary bigfoot that people claimed existed but nobody knew how to find. 

It was a real shame too, because unless Jane missed her guess, anyplace that secret had to be where the Bible Study was hiding as well.

Revisiting Melinda, Jane learned that the only thing the lady did like clockwork was to attend the early service at church with some old friends.  

Jane's mouth drew itself out in a dubious expression.  It wasn't much to go off, and the woman would be easy to miss in the large crowd.  There was no other alternative though.

Instead of attending the late service with Misty, Jane now had a sneaky plan.  Now it was her turn to play secret agent.  She milled about the parking lot keeping a close eye on the entrance.  One advantage of her overactive senses was that her mind often caught and filed away helpful details.  Right now she was looking for one of the cars in particular from the night of the first Bible Study.  She was positive she'd recognize it if she saw it again.  The encounter with Wendell all but proved that she could do it too.

The plan was simple.  She didn't expect to talk with Melinda at church.  She'd only get more of Misty's, "I can't tell you", business.  No, her plan was to silently and sneakily follow her to whatever secret place she was going.  It might take all day but eventually Melinda was going to lead her to the very doorstep of the Bible Study itself!

This was a great plan!

So far nothing coming or going in the parking lot looked familiar though.  Jane perked up when she saw a black Mercedes SUV pull in.  But her heart sank again when she realized it was Michael's car, the identical twin to Melinda's.  

He saw her and waved.  

Jane waved back.  Conceivably she could go after him again, but it wasn't ideal.  She'd somehow gotten the picture that she was supposed to touch bases with everybody.  Ms McCready had hinted that this was some kind of test, hadn't she?

The first service started, but still no Melinda.  Jane decided to file in with the rest and at least enjoy the music.  This was unfortunate, for had she decided to play hooky that morning, she would have seen and recognized the second black Mercedes that pulled into the parking lot just after her back was turned.  It circled around back of the building and tucked itself discreetly among the dumpsters.

After the service, Jane was about to take back up her post near the parking lot entrance when she caught a fleeting glimpse of somebody walking out the back doors.  It was Melinda Moast!  There was no question!

...So we meet again, Mister Bond...  It was a silly thought.   And then she bolted out the front doors.

Jane's little white Toyota was strategically parked along the curb and ready to make a fast getaway.  No sooner was the tiny Japanese engine purring and ready to roll, then she saw the black SUV pull out into traffic.  She shifted into drive and began to follow.

Had she been acting like a real life agent rather than a movie one she wouldn't have been following as closely as she was.  Not to mention when the Moast car suddenly made a left turn into a vacant store's parking lot, she wouldn't have been dumb enough to follow directly behind it.

But now her cover was blown.

The driver of the black car immediately slammed the gas pedal all the way down and squealed the tires.  Jane also accelerated - less rapidly, of course, but as fast as the little car could.  Her only thought was that she didn't want to lose Melinda.  If her concentration was focused more on the road instead she might've seen the speed bump before it was too late.  

The suspension of the SUV was designed for boulders and took it with ease.  The little Toyota went momentarily airborne.  The car slammed down hard.  Jane's teeth rattled and it felt like she left her stomach behind sometime around takeoff. 

The SUV decided to make a hasty exit of the parking lot but didn't wait for a driveway.  It bounced over a dividing island instead.  Then its driver slammed the wheel in a hard left, chirping the tires, and swerving sideways back onto the main road.  

Like a faithful lemming, Jane jumped the curb right after it.  Her muffler stayed behind.

The SUV roared up a freeway on-ramp nearby, passed a semi on the shoulder and cut back in front of it.  

Jane did the same before she realized the terrible risk involved.  The resounding hoot of the truck's huge air horns made her skin crawl as she realized how close she'd come to getting smeared across the asphalt by twenty tons worth of semi.

The black SUV weaved in and out of traffic with all the skillful ease of a professional NASCAR driver.

Jane tried to keep up for a few more miles, goaded on by Misty's words, "Don't give up!"  She could hear them even now.

The more powerful car and the more skillful driver slowly put distance between the two.  

Moreover, Jane began to realize the incredible danger of the pursuit.  High speed chases in the movies were exciting and downright cool.  In real life they were a good way to get dead.  The chance of success was dim and the risk was chillingly real.  

Jane reluctantly decided to break off the chase.

She eased off the throttle and let the speedometer fall back to a rational speed.  She slowly drifted down the next off ramp, muttering a soft, "Sorry Misty.  I tried."


Thoroughly defeated, Jane made her way back to the church to meet Misty.  

Once inside the building Jane headed straight for the ladies room to wash up.  Her hands were still shaking and her face was red and sweating.  She grabbed a paper towel, wet it, and shakily mopped her face with it.  Her heart was still beating like a hummingbird.  

She walked back out to the lobby.  There she resumed watching for other members of the Bible Study.  There was some thought of trying the plan again.  (This time the following would have to be done with more attention to stealth.)  

Jane was now forming a love/hate relationship with her plan.  The thing with Melinda was supposed to have been a matter of quietly following somebody to wherever she was going -- not a high-speed car chase!  Her nerves couldn't stand a second round of that game.  She was reluctant to even try again for fear of a repeat performance.  

In fact, her knees were still wobbly from last time.

Jane leaned against the surface of the "welcome booth" for support.  The girl behind it was talking on the desk phone, answering somebody's questions about service times.  Whoever was on the other end must have left a message because the girl pulled open a drawer beneath the phone and fumbled for a pen.

She hung up and dashed off to deliver whatever the message was the same moment Jane heard the voice of Misty behind her.  Jane's mind processed through all this happening at once and balked slightly.  Then she turned to face Misty.

Misty greeted her warmly.  "Looks like you beat me here." she said.  Then she saw Jane's red hot face and her expression changed to one of concern.  "Are you okay?"

"Yeah.  I'm fine."  But her thoughts were on the phone, not Misty.

"Wanna go find a seat, then?"

Jane nodded.  As Misty turned to walk towards the auditorium, Jane quickly reached over the top of the counter and slipped her hand into the drawer.  She felt what she was after, grabbed it, and quickly stuffed it into her bag.

Misty realized that her friend had fallen behind and turned just as she hurried up to her side.

"Are you sure you're 'fine'?"

Jane smiled.  "Don't give up." she quoted.

Misty cocked an eyebrow, not sure what to make of that.  But then she smiled and gave an approving nod.  She took her friend's arm and together they walked into the auditorium.

Jane had already heard the sermon once before.  Then she had overexerted her herself.   On top of that, she had also given herself quite the workout this week.  All three things joined forces against her.  

She slept like a baby in the pew.


~18~
Conference Call

The robot's plastic skull lay on the table.  Its lifeless eyes stared forward unmoving and a tangle of wires trailed from it like electronic entrails.

For all intents it looked like a dead thing.  And yet a small red LED light blinked on a circuit board next to it, giving it at least some indication of life.  It contained no hope or soul with it however, but looked more like a Terminator about to rise from the grave one more time.

The skull stared unblinkingly across the desk at an ancient cream-colored office phone ringing away.  It was touch-tone, but only just barely.  Everything about it screamed 1960's.  That quaint appearance was deceptive however, since it was also backed by a state-of-the-art Asterisk PBX host which routed SIP digital communication over a forked global satellite uplink.

A hand sporting several band-aids reached out and grabbed the bakelite receiver.  "Ready?" it said.  And then, "Just a moment.  I'll bring them in."

The finger tapped out a series of keys.
[speaker][conf.][speed dial 2][conf.][speed dial 3][conf.][speed dial 4]

The hand retracted from the keypad and began to work on the robot with a small screwdriver while the additional calls rang and were answered in succession.

"Everybody here?" said a masculine voice.

The others all, in turn, repeated, "Here."

After that, he continued, "You know what this is about.  What do you think?"  And without waiting for a reply cast his own vote, "Personally, with that much spunk, I'd say go for it."

"Agreed." said a female voice on the same line.

"You know where I stand." said a faint, squeaky voice.

"Shurin' I wouldn't object." said another.

"Well, she owes me a pizza.  But other than that, I'm good with it."

The screwdriver in the robot owner's hand froze.  "Really?  Is this what it comes down to nowdays?"

"What do you mean?" asked one of the other voices.

"What I mean is that we have a certain procedure for this sort of thing.  And we all agreed on what we needed ahead of time.  That's important.

"You all know the policy:  We always seek out recruits instead of having them find us.  Let me remind you, there's a good reason why that is.  Doing it the other way means we set ourselves up for a great risk of infiltration!

"We might as well hang a big welcome sign on the door that says 'Secret meeting here, Sundays 6 pm.  Terrorists and KGB welcome'!"

"I can vouch for her." testified the small voice of the group.

"Not good enough." said the robot man again.

"You know that this has to be unanimous, right?" said the first voice he'd talked to.

"Then it's not unanimous." he declared with finality.  They all remembered hearing that tone from him before and knew that further argument was useless.

So that was the end of it.  Slowly, one by one, the other callers each bid a melancholy and disappointed, "goodbye", and dropped off the line.  All but one.  The soft-voice.

She knew as well as the others that argument would do nothing, so she asked a question instead.  "What would it take for you to give her a chance?"

He thought about it for a moment.  "Since you put it that way...  If she wants in, she has to somehow impress me well enough to justify it."

And then he added a note of warning.  "But the rest of you had better stay out of it, or the deal is off."

"I see.  Thank you."

And then the last call dropped.

The man was finally left alone, happily, to his robot.


~19~
Secret Meeting

Jane's old and mufflerless Toyota roared up in front of an abandoned fish processing plant in a questionable section of town not far from the docks.  

She saw the address she was looking for painted in faded numbers over a door on one extreme corner of the building.  She had to hand it to the Bible Study.  It was a great hiding place.

The structure was a three-story building made of orange brick held tenuously in place with crumbling mortar.  In fact, one entire end was covered with plastic sheeting.   In several places within the bottom six feet the walls were grafittid, painted over, and grafittid again.   

Several huge glass windows ran down the length of the building.  Each spanned nearly the entire height before being topped off by an arched half circle.  Many of their small panes were cracked over the years by vandalous kids with rocks and bb guns.  A pity.  This was once a fine building.

The place had an odd artfulness to it that a factory builder wouldn't even consider today.  Cold, concrete rectangle - that's what a modern builder would've put on the site.  But back in the 1930's when this was built, architects were more artist and less accountant.

Jane's imagination could have a field day imagining the way it looked back when it was new.  No time for that now though.

The encounters with Ms McCready and Michel Moast gave her a certain sense of encouragement.  The encounters with Wendell and Melinda Moast, however, resulted in a less positive message.  Who were these strange people and did they want her around or not?

She knew that Misty did, but that's because they were best friends.  The others - were they just patronizing her for Misty's sake?  It sure felt like it.

And now that Jane finally figured out the secret to where they were hiding, what then?  Would they welcome her in, or push her away?

The various emotions had rattled around inside her all the way over to this place.  Eventually the one that won control was a heavy sense of indignation.  Regardless of whether they really wanted her here or not, there was definitely a level of disrespect she'd been forced to endure.  Going around toying with a person like this must be downright unreasonable.

Yes, indignation was what it all came down to.  She'd at least pop in long enough to prove that she found them and to give these smart alecks a piece of her mind.

Jane gritted her teeth and narrowed her eyes.  She stormed up to the door.  She rapped hard on it.  

She was unsurprised when a moment later the familiar face of Dr Puttery appeared.

His expression was one of bewilderment at first.  It slowly dissolved into one of steadfast determination.  He clearly was not about to let her inside as evidenced by his outstretched arm leaning against the door sill and blocking entrance.  That figured.  He began to fumble for words to come up with some contrived excuse as to why she should leave.  

Wendell was just beyond him, looking down at his own feet.

Jane didn't know what to say.  She hated this.  She hated being the outsider.  Hated the clique.  She honestly wouldn't have minded leaving the selfish brats to their own private party except for one thing that lured her into all this.

That one thing materialized before her eyes -- the face of Misty.

"Hello!" she beamed. "I knew you'd make it!"

The short Misty easily darted underneath Dr Puttery's outstretched arm, grabbed Jane, and dragged her inside.  Jane was so much taller than Misty that she was forced to duck fast to avoid being clotheslined by Puttery's arm.

Dr Puttery stood at the door grunting and sputtering in his own indignation.

"I sure hope she knows what she's doing." said Wendell.  His rare moment of seriousness added enormous weight to the words.

"Well, we'll just have to see about that, won't we?"  Dr Puttery slowly closed the door and walked inside.

On the inside, the abandoned fish plant bore the resemblance of... well... an abandoned fish plant.  It was a dusty warehouse about 75 feet wide and who knows how many feet deep.  The far end was lost completely in hazy blackness.  A few mercury vapor spotlights hung down from the ceiling by their cords.  Most had working bulbs but many did not.

Where they shone, surplus steel desks, and dining room tables, and doors propped up on sawhorses, and a variety of other things created work surfaces.  Upon each was a random assortment of books, half-dismantled machines, lab equipment, and, Jane noted with a look of rising apprehension, more than one stack of guns.

What is this place? she thought.

One section, maybe 20 feet from the door was a seating area.  It wasn't defined by any kind of solid walls but more by a grid of fluorescent fixtures whose light painted the square room out of the darkness.

It contained several mismatched couches which couldn't have been more opposite those of Melinda Moast's house.  Hers were leather, soft, and expensive, whereas these looked like they came off a curbside before the garbage truck could get to them.  Several had been repaired with duct tape.  Others needed to be.

Misty dragged Jane towards the familiar faces.  All turned to see Jane and the conversation died away into an awkward silence.  

"She's found us!  Isn't it great?" she beamed.

Nobody agreed.

The look on their faces radiated various levels of un-wantedness back at the interloper.  It was actually about the un-wantedness of an ugly fight they all felt brewing, but Jane interpreted it as a personal snubbing.

If her civility was to have even a chance here she needed something, anything, to grasp onto.  She knew what it would have to be and looked for the kindly face of Ms McCready.  Their eyes met.  McCready's eyes were not unfriendly, but sad.  She slunk backwards a step and looked down.

So that's how it's going to be then?

Jane's emotional pot began to boil over.  She'd played the part of the unwanted outcast before and didn't take kindly to it.  Moreover, she had a temper and she knew it.  (As her past shouting arguments with God and others could attest.)  

In fact, if she'd have been popular, or at least been able to get along with those who were, she wouldn't even be here in California, and wouldn't be standing here with these... these...
"JERKS!" she shouted the last of the thought.

It echoed through the cavernous space.  Far too late to take it back, Jane gave full vent to her anger.  "What's the big idea with all this hiding business, huh?"

"We weren't..." protested Michael weakly.

"Don't give me that!" shouted Jane back, "I know when I'm given the cold shoulder!  You've all got your cozy little group, or club, or 'faction' thingy going on here and you don't want to let me in!  Well, fine!  I know when I'm not wanted!  You can just have your dumb old - whatever it is!  And who needs ya!"

Her tirade at an end, she turned to storm off, but found that she suddenly couldn't walk.  Misty was literally hanging off her waist, bawling.

"But she found the meeting..." she appealed up at Dr Puttery in between sobs.

Mumbling to himself, he allowed that the fact was, "somewhat impressive".  "But", he was quick to add, "not enough to become a permanent member of the team."  

His self-discussion was over.  His stone expression, unmoved.

"But it's dangerous..." he retorted.

"But she can help..." said Misty.

"But she's a photographer..."  He cast an accusing glance at Wendell as he said it.

"But..." Misty began her next plea.

She never finished.  During the exchange, Ms McCready had navigated herself behind Puttery, and now spoke a few soft words that ended the conversation.

"...but why can't we a' least give her a chance?" she asked.

It was a hard piece of logic to get around.  After all, how could he expect her to prove herself without giving her the opportunity?

"Fine.  One chance." he relented.  Then he shot Misty a sideways glance.  "But she better impress me or that's the end of it."

Everyone else in the room breathed a sigh of relief at the tense situation having been defused.

Jane's mind did back flips.
So they were going to let her in, then?  Did she even want to be in?  For that matter, what exactly was she in?  And "dangerous"?  What was that supposed to mean?

Jane couldn't process it.  She merely let Misty drag her to one of the couches.  Sitting.  Yes.  That's a good idea.  Maybe if she sat she could collect her wits and make some sense of what was going on.


~20~
The First Assignment

Everyone took a seat.  

Jane was led to a patched couch and sank down next to Misty with gentle tugging.  The group was all too glad to put the fight behind them, but Jane's adrenaline was still running high and made her not-so-quick to forgive.  Still, she knew if she could somehow keep calm at least she could manage a civilized, adult conversation.  

And who knows?  Maybe if she listened there would be some reasonable explanation about why they behaved like stuck-up, conceited, juvenile...  She took a deep breath and let it out.  Calm...  calm...

Melinda Moast slowly made the rounds, handing out a manila envelope to each member.  Jane noticed with some puzzlement that there were the precise number needed for everyone to have one, including her.  Had Melinda somehow known that Jane would find them tonight?  She might have to give the prissy heiress more credit in the future.

Prissy?  No. she corrected herself.  Melinda Moast was precise and exacting to a fault, but it didn't culminate in 'prissy', but more 'formidable'.  

"Messieur Benoit Rousseau," began Michael Moast.  Each opened his or her envelope and removed an 8x10 photo.

Jane did likewise and dropped it with a, "Wah!"

The photo was of a pale corpse with a tortured look on its face.

Nobody else reacted similarly, nor took much notice of her reaction.

"French Ambassador to the United States," continued Micheal.  "Missing 18 days, body picked up by a fishing vessel off Stanton Island this morning."

"Call... the... policeeeee..." said Wendell, no doubt making some obscure pop-culture reference that nobody got but him.  He laughed anyway.

"Yes.  Well." continued Michael, "The trouble isn't so much that there was a high-level assassination, but the way he died."

He paused for effect.

"The autopsy is still in progress, but initial reports are calling for 'Mollusk Poisoning'."

Nobody said anything.  Nobody knew what to say about that subject.  Well, except for the one person in the room who knew what to say about nearly any subject.

"Mollusks?  Well, you see," began Dr Puttery, "the thing about biologists is they like to come up with classifications.  Classify this, classify that, you know.  And every first year Biology student is given neat and tidy little family trees of animals to memorize, like that's the beginning and the end of it. When in reality..."

He looked at his glass, contemplating for a moment.
"...nature is messy.  A little of this creature here, a little of that one there, and 'Oh my!  I don't even know what that thing is!'  That's basically what the category of 'mollusk' means.  It's a grab bag for creatures (mostly sea-dwelling ones) that nobody knows what else to do with, from squids to clams."

"Don't even get him started on 'plankton'." whispered Wendell from across the coffee table.  The whisper was intended for its comedy effect, not for its secrecy.  

Dr Puttery clearly heard it, and it did indeed set him off.  "Plankton could be ANYTHING!" he shouted, waggling a finger high in the air.

Micheal moved in swiftly to cut off the irrelevant rant in its infancy.  "In this particular case, all we know from the words 'Mollusk Poison', is that a chemical analysis program matched a sample against its database.  It decided it looked the most similar to something from some random creepy-crawly in the vast oceans of the world somewhere."

"That sounds a little vague." understated Melinda Moast.

"It is.  More importantly though," added Micheal, "it matches a rash of similar assassinations that have been happening more and more frequently over the last six months.  All of the world's surveillance organizations are climbing the walls for lack of intel' on this one.  Every one of them is wondering who's guy is going to suddenly keel over next.  Yet they haven't the slightest clue what's happening or how to prevent it."

"Fun." said Wendell.

"It gets worse.  Through murmurings I've heard from my sources in the underground, the killers may even have bigger mayhem than that in mind.  The plans they have in the works go well beyond a few political celebrities floating face down in New York Harbor.  I hate to say it, but we're looking at the possibility of mass murder on a national or even international scale here!

"They could do it too.  From the tests the forensics lab has been performing, this particular poison is horribly lethal stuff.  Even the trace amount they found in the Ambassador's bloodstream would be potent enough to easily wipe out all life in a 5 mile radius!  Worse still it can be absorbed through the skin, so mere gas masks would be useless against it.  (The poor researcher who learned that fact is still in critical condition!)

"To put it plain and simple, team, whoever deals in such things is not just a common, garden-variety murderer, but a downright psychotic megalomaniac.  Before they can do anything worse, they must be stopped!"

"But how?" asked his wife.

He turned to face her.  "Whatever this new poison is, the baddies are obviously getting it from somewhere special.  Find the source and we find the killers.  That's our job."

The speech was at an end.  The room was silent in contemplation for a long time.  Everybody was digesting the puzzle they'd been so suddenly presented with.  

Everyone, that is, except for Jane who was still back at the beginning, contemplating what this group was, anyway.  All of the clues gathered so far pointed to some sort of secret underground cadre of international do-gooders posing as a mild-mannered Bible Study.

She was strangely calm about that realization.  Possibly because it made so little sense that her brain had nothing else to associate it with.

"Pass." said Dr Puttery finally.

"Reason?" asked Michel Moast.

"It's just that jobs involving the briny blue sea have never really been our strong suit.  Except for a few coast-guard terrorist hijackings here and there, our only other ocean job was..." he hesitated to say it, "Atlantis."

The room erupted in argument.

Up until this point Jane's senses had been recovering from the earlier episode.  In fact, they were nearly back to normal.  Now they began their familiar swirl as her eyes darted from person to person in the hubbub.  Her mind tried to soak in all of everything, and so, delivered back very little of nearly nothing.

"I was down so long, me fingers are still pruned." Ms McCready looked mournfully at her stubby fingers.

Wendell stormed off into the shadows, returned with part of a busted orange vause and waved it around, shouting something Jane couldn't get.

"No, no, no, no." said the unseen voice of Melinda Moast.

Dr Puttery roughly shoved Wendell toward a free-standing chalkboard and made him draw a picture of something.

Even Misty jumped up and down and gesticulated wildly.  Being small of body and voice she was neither really seen nor heard by anyone in the commotion.

Jane could make no sense out of any of it.  She fervently wished it would all...

"STOP!" boomed the commanding voice of Michel Moast, taking the words right out of her mouth.

That, she understood.  As did everyone else.

"This is not about Atlantis." he decreed.

"You." he said, pointing directly at Wendell. "This is NOT about Atlantis."

"But what if they're using Atlantis to farm the mollusks to..."

"No." said Michel, visibly tightening the muscles to his outstretched finger.

In one single powerfully-delivered word he'd produced an order, a decision, a threat, and declared the matter closed, all at once.  

"Doctor," said Misty after the clamor died down, "You said yourself that 'mollusk poison' could mean almost anything from anywhere?"

"Correct, my dear." replied Puttery.

"Well," continued the timid girl, "If it could be anything from anywhere, then realistically the best we can do is a high-level sweep and see if anything funky turns up.  

"The plan is, we split up, see?  We simply borrow three or four multispectral molecular interferometers from your biology associates, slap them in some old torpedo casings from Ms McCready's sock drawer, and drag them behind the Moast's hydroplane yachts."

"No good." responded Dr Puttery, "The world's seas are about 1.3 billion cubic kilometers of water!  It would take... "

He did the math.  "...over 40 years to do what you're asking."

"We'd run out of Ambassadors before then." chimed in Wendell, uselessly.

Michael sounded close to a decision.  "You're right, of course Dr Puttery.  But if this stuff is as toxic as they say, it might indeed be potent enough to pick up.

"If we were to split up, that would help.  Then we'd also eliminate areas like rugged, deepwater, and frigid seas..."

"Reefs!" Dr Puttery snapped his fingers.  His face lost its sour expression as well.

"Mind explaining that?" asked Michael.

"Coral Reefs make up a comparatively small portion of the ocean's floor, but the abundance of life forms is over twelve thousand times denser there.  It's no guarantee, mind you, (a one in four chance perhaps) but if you want the best bet for finding something, those odds are pretty hard to beat!"

Michael cut to the chase.  "So, say we decided to split up and just hit the major reefs.  Not an extended stay, mind you, just sweep for poison and move on.  How long to do that?"

Dr Puttery shrugged.  "Within those parameters the seven of us here could make a rough tour of the world's oceans in, say, a couple of months."

"Well, that's the plan then.  We take a month or two, explore the reefs for traces of the toxin.  If we don't find anything, that's that.  Agreed?"  Thus Michael called for the vote.

"Dr Puttery?" he asked, officially.

"I'm in now."

"In that case, we're both in." said Melinda Moast, speaking for the both of them.

"Misty?"

"In."

"Ms McCready?"

"In."

"Wendell?"

"Roger-dodger, chief."

He hesitated and asked a little deeper question of Jane.

"You do realize you could be in a thousand kinds of danger if you go along with this hair-brained plan?  Are you prepared to join us?"

Jane couldn't believe what she was considering becoming a part of: sea mollusks... assassins... cloak and dagger...  Insanity!  She was even more surprised when she heard herself answer with an emphatic, "Absolutely!"

"Yay!" cheered Misty quietly.  She squeezed Jane's arm as she said it, as if they had just gotten permission to attend a pajama party rather than embarking a globetrotting and potentially lethal wild goose chase.


~21~
Dawn

As Misty and Jane walked out of the strange warehouse, they were surprised to see that the pink rays of dawn were already forming in the sky.  The "Bible Study" had lasted all night.

The dawn air had a particular nip to it, but smelled fantastic after the permeating scent of fish and dust inside.

Misty spoke first.  "I heard how you tracked most of the group down.  That was some real good work for a beginner!  But even I was surprised when I heard you tried to chase down Melinda.  That was real dangerous!  The Moasts have both taken police driving courses."  And then after a moment, "I hope your car's okay."

Jane only shrugged.  "But she got away so..."

"So?"

Jane remembered what Michael had said about failure back in the gym.  "Yeah, you're right."

Misty continued.  "I heard about all that, but one thing is still bothering me.  I'm dying to know:  How did you find this place, anyway?  It's about the best kept secret in town!"

"The town's not much for secrets, then."  Jane smiled and held up the thick booklet she'd filched from the church welcome table.  "Church Directory:  'Puttery, Eugene M.' "

Misty's riotous laughter echoed down the empty early morning streets.  She tried to comment a couple of times but she was laughing too hard to be understandable.  Jane laughed too.  It felt good being able to laugh again, now that the tension and frustration of the past week was finally resolved.

As the humor of two giddy girls wound down, Jane stopped to look at the time on her pink phone.  "Oh man!  Not even time for nap before work."

As the word "work" sank in, she froze in mid-step.

"Work!  Oh no!  I can't run off searching the seven seas for mollusks!  I'll lose my job!"
Misty giggled so hard she clutched her friend's arm to steady herself.
