"What the--"  He never finished the thought.  Somebody else working on the sabotage saw the bronze tube of the periscope extend and decided to kick it back down.  The blow slammed Enzo to the floor by the eye sockets.  

He rubbed his face and cursed.

In between the foul oaths he was able to bark out.  "Send some men out there and kill those idiots before they can do any more damage!"

In doing so, he unwittingly set the timer of Jane's plan in motion.

The strategy began paying off all over the battlefield.  The Atlanticans swarmed the enemy subs like flies on a rotten zucchini.  Each time the divers were sent out in response, they faded back and waited for air supply to win the skirmish for them.

Several recovered minisubs were now involved, thanks to the Bible Study team playing mechanic.  Indeed, there were only two subs that hadn't been a simple fix and both were set aside in favor of the many easy repairs piling up.

Listening to the background chatter on the Atlantican communication set,  one voice stood out,  "Hey Sarge, we had a group of Sea Dragons run nearly out of air, but they took refuge in one of the city buildings.  Should we go in after 'em?"  

A voice, presumably the man's Sergeant, replied,  "No.  Without their own air they'll be trapped inside.  Better keep beating on those subs until they figure out a way to defeat our strategy."

His words proved prophetic.  Just at that moment Enzo was supervising the loading of one of his prized canisters of mollusk poison into a torpedo tube.  Unlike a torpedo, it had no propulsion with it.  It didn't need to.  He didn't plan on it going anywhere, but instead leaking out from right where it was.

Jane was horrified when she saw it happen.  Several Atlantican divers surrounding the flagship began shouting and choking over the communication system.  She ghasped.  The others looked up from their work to see the men convulsing and thrashing the same way that the poor Atlantican on the Starfish had done.

"Get back everybody!  Get back!  They're releasing the poison!" someone shouted.  Most of the Atlantican warriors were able to escape to a safe distance, but all too many were not.

The aftermath of Jane's plan was hard to tally.  There was no way of telling just how crippled the nuclear subs were, but all of them were certainly handicapped in at least some way.  Quite a number of the enemy SCUBA force was also floating limply around the battlefield.

Even those Sea Dragon divers that weren't already dead or trapped inside the buildings of Atlantis were effectively out of the battle now as well.  The clouds of poison that now hung around the huge subs made exit of any kind impossible.  

Furthermore, what few of the subs were still physically capable of aiming, were down to only one or two torpedo tubes due to poison contamination in the rest.  Sitting ducks for the Starfish to pick off!

All in all, Jane's idea certainly evened up the score a bit.

The job of the Atlantican soldiers now shifted focus back to the matter of capturing minisubs.  Their tactics were not ideal, but they slowly made progress.  

Thanks to the Bible Study, their results would be exponential, however, since each sub they disabled was confiscated and sent back for repair.  It was like a zombie movie, where each person who was bitten got added to the enemy's numbers.  

For the first time it seemed like the war might actually wind up in their favor in the end!


Many Generals would have naively stayed the course and watched their army slowly melt away like an ice cube on the sidewalk.  Enzo Fontana was not the sort to give up so easily.

"How many have we restored contact with?" he demanded.

"All but four." the communications operator replied.

"Show me."

The man used the tip of a mechanical pencil to point at several sub icons flashing on a radar screen.  "These three, and this one over here."

A sour look of contemplation hung on Enzo's face for a few seconds.  Then he spoke again.  "Order all responding SSN's heave-to alongside us.  We'll form a line here at the edge of the city.  We'll start high and use our poison clouds to drive those fools across the city and into the other subs.

"Have the minis sweep in from the sides so nobody can sneak past and escape.  I mean to crush 'em in a trash compactor made out of pure poison!"

One of the icons winked out at that moment.

"And for crying out loud!  Somebody figure out a way to stop that sub-eating monster out there before it kills us all!"

The Atlantican Generals had no problem seeing the movement in the fleet and realize something was up.  Once the formation became evident, it didn't take the quick-witted leaders long to figure out what it meant.  There was nothing that could be done about it though.  Nobody could go near the enemy without dying and the Starfish couldn't cut through that many subs fast enough to prevent the inevitable.

The enemy formation was complete, even now.  With an unheard word from their leader, the line of subs began slowly advancing on the Atlantican force.  The noose was tightening around their necks...  and there was nowhere to run!


~55~
Bomb Technicians

Nagorno Karabakh was one of the smallest countries in the world.  It would be difficult to place it on any kind of 'top ten' list though.  When it comes places that small, it gets difficult to even decide what constitutes 'a country', anway.  

If Nagorno Karabakh were to join the United States, it would automatically be the third smallest one, right between Delaware and Connecticut.  No wonder few other countries bothered to recognize it.

For the most part, the place was just as happy not being recognized.  The few times it had attracted outside attention it was invariably a bad thing.  There was always some big kid on the block that saw Nagorno Karabakh as easy pickings.  Still, it had somehow always managed to outlive its oppressors.  Its identity had even survived through the time when the Soviet Union held iron-fisted control of the entire region.

The country's former USSR occupation was the very reason that Jack and Rurick now sped along its serene, misty mountain roads in an old rented Land Cruiser.

The decrepit vehicle was a good off-roader back in its day.  Though that day was long past, it might do a respectable job even yet.  

Jack had hated to leave his battered Humvee behind in Mongolia.  Yet that couldn't be helped since it was about two thousand miles from there to Nagorno Karabakh as the crow flies.  Without a crow it's much longer still as there were several mountain ranges to cross as well as the Caspian Sea.

And so, Jack had kissed the poor baby on the hood and swapped it for a battered old Cessna 140 bush plane - a throwback to the 1940's.  The man he'd made the deal with was reluctant to let his plane go due to its usefulness in the remote area.  He was easily persuaded though once he added it up and realized that he was getting the better end of the deal, swapping the plane for a vehicle worth five times as much.

For sentimental reasons Jack kept the sunglasses and the serial number plate.  Dr Puttery would hopefully find the latter useful in tracking down who'd been getting all cuddly with the rebels.

It was a long, long flight in the old bird.  Jack's rear end had long ago gone numb.  Worse still, every hour that ticked by meant the army of the Czar-wannabe were that much closer to their intended target.

They'd gone straight from the plane to the road and the many weary hours of traveling were beginning to show on both men.

Jack's head strained to see a sign whipping past.  "What was that?  Was that our turn?"

"No.  'Hoghi hankyer'."

"What's that?"

"Warning: Land mines."

"Great.  Land mines.  That's just great."  he complained.

"That's our destination."  Rurick pointed out the windshield and up at a mountain peak.  "The Anechnya complex, is an old, abandoned Soviet missile silo left over from the early days of the Cold War - a very strategic position for one, too!  From there they could hit anything from England to India!"

"And how do you know about it?" asked Jack.

"I was present when they beat it out of a prisoner."

"I'm sorry I asked."

"Me too."  Between glances at the passenger, Jack could see that there was no small amount of regret written on the Russian's face.  

They drove on in silence for a few more minutes.

"There's a clearing." said Rurick.  It was a happy change of subject but one that confused Jack.

Jack did as he was directed, but voiced his puzzlement.  "I thought we were driving up to Anechnya?"

Rurick was already dragging his backpack from the back seat.  "No.  Not driving."  He got out and slammed the door behind him.

Jack snatched up his own pack and ran to where Rurick was already beginning to tackle the steep slope.

"But what about the... hoagie... hoagie sandwich... um...  The land mines, anyway?"

Rurick turned and looked him in the eye.  "I advise against stepping on them." he said simply.  He gave a wink and resumed climbing.

Jack muttered something sarcastic under his breath as he followed.


It was a long and steep climb with no trail.  Dark fully settled by the time they finally reached a clearing large and flat enough to camp on.  

Jack realized with some surprise that it wasn't just flat enough.  It was perfectly flat.

Up until now the two had been hiking without artificial light.  It was an old woodcraft trick that they both knew.  If you used a flashlight, you'd ruin your night-vision but if you toughed it out instead you'd ultimately be able to see farther.

Now there was something too strange about the area.  Jack decided it was worth trading his night-vision to find out what it was.  He flicked on the light.  He was standing on a giant letter H.

He shined the light around more.  The letter was painted on concrete and half-obscured by what looked like dry leaves.

He knelt down and touched them.  They weren't leaves at all but instead the fabric weave of very old and half-rotten camouflage netting.

"If this is the helipad, then the entrance must be nearby." observed Rurick.

Even though it was pushing midnight, both were too anxious to leave the search for morning.  Without a word they each took a light and began searching the uphill slope in each direction.

"I've got it!" called Jack.

Rurick came running.  Jack was standing in front of a concrete-lined alcove cut into the cliff.  Its entrance was piled high with boulders.

"The Soviets must have buried it before they left."

Rurick nodded.  He then added, "Look at the top.  There's a gap."

The two scrambled up the side of the hill and then lowered themselves carefully down onto the stack of boulders.  The gap between the topmost rock and the concrete door frame was just enough for a man to crawl through.

Just inside Jack noticed something red/orange reflected by his flashlight.  He got closer and touched it.  There was a solid metal door on the other side of the rocks.  An entrance hole had been crudely cut through it with a torch.  From the rust scale on the cut he judged that it must've happened many years ago.

"Do you think they stole the missiles?" asked Jack.  It wasn't long before he regretted the question.

Rurick gave him that look again.  "How could they get an entire ballistic missile out of that hole?"

One at a time they lowered themselves through the opening and dropped to the floor inside.  The room they entered was a long, dark, spiderweb-filled corridor.  It stretched far off into the distance -- much farther than their flashlight beams could reach.  

Jack felt that the whole place had peculiar eerie gloom about it.  If it was a thousand year old Aztec temple he might have even considered it 'cursed'.  As it was from a relatively recent age in history and built by people not much different than he or Rurick, the thought seemed strangely incongruous.  Still, the darkness seemed almost like a living thing as it pressed in on him.

The sound of the two mens' footsteps reverberated easily off every surface of the concrete tube and echoed far off into the vast distance ahead.

After walking for what seemed like miles, something finally broke the monotony of the corridor.  It was a door off to the left.  Jack made as if to begin exploring it.

Rurick placed a hand on his shoulder.  "Don't bother.  That'll be the barracks.  The missile silo will be nearer to the center of the mountain.  But it means we're getting close at least."

He was right, as usual.  The tunnel ended with a double set of steel blast doors.  Each had been cut from its hinges and tossed aside.

They walked into a computer control room.  Windows lined the walls to either side, but outside them was nothing but inky blackness.  Old beige computer terminals stood in neat rows in front of each.  

A pair of reel-to-reel tape drives stood like refrigerators on the back wall.  All was stone dead, but apart from the dust it all looked exactly the same as the day it was abandoned.

An empty coffee cup even sat next to a terminal.  Its contents had long since evaporated, leaving only a dark stain behind.

"Do you want to get some sleep or should we get started?" asked Rurick.

"As soon as dawn hits we're down to our last 12 hours and I'd like to give us as wide a margin as possible.  On the other hand, I hate to be groggy if I'm going to play rocket scientist."

"How about this plan:  You get some work done while I sleep for three or four hours and then we trade places."

"Good idea.  While you're napping I'll see if I can find the generators and get this old junk powered up."

Rurick unrolled his sleeping bag on the hard floor while Jack went off in search of the generators.


Three hours later, almost to the minute, Rurick was rudely awakened by a distant clunk and an intensely bright light in his face.  He spat something derogatory in Russian.

"Sorry about that." said Jack.  He was standing at a metal wall panel in the corner with one hand still on a circuit breaker.  "I forgot to check the light switch first."

He flipped another breaker.  The tape drives began to chatter and click like electronic chipmunks as the text terminals started scrolling green Cyrillic characters.

Though Jack had learned to pronounce the words they formed, Dr Puttery's history lessons made the characters seem strange all over again.  Cyrillic, the printed language of one of the most violently Atheist nations of all time -- yet also a living legacy of the work of a pair of Bible translators and missionaries, Cyril and Methodious.

He flipped another breaker and flood lights came on outside the windows illuminating the top sections of huge ballistic missiles.

The next breaker did nothing perceptible.

Neither did the following two.

Jack reasoned that everything here was probably important in some way, and made sure to power them all on.

Having finished the power-up cycle, Jack walked to a window and admired the size of the terrible weapon on the other side.  After a moment he noticed something that made his heart sink.  "They're busted!" he lamented aloud.

"What?"  Rurick leapt to his feet and ran to the window.  The nose cone of the missile was gone.  "Oh good.  I expected that much.  They were probably dismantled by terrorists.  When we find the shells, we'll no doubt find all the enriched uranium stolen."

"Terrorists stole the nuclear material...  and that's considered 'good'?"  Jack was skeptical.

"Do you know how to defuse a nuclear warhead?"

There was no answer, so he continued.  "Well, neither do I.  And I wasn't looking forward to learning how on only three hours of sleep.

"Speaking of which, it's your turn now."

Jack was exhausted but sleep at a time like this was the last thing on his mind.  It was a force of willpower to get himself to go under.

When he awoke later, Rurick was nowhere to be seen.

He walked to the window and peered out.  Not there.

He walked to the other.  His friend was hunched over a nearly assembled warhead casing on the silo floor.


A moment later Jack walked up behind him.

He jumped.

"Oh, it's you Jack.  You startled me."  Rurick waved him closer.  "Maybe you can help.  You see that little red thing?  And the one over there?"  He gestured with the tip of a screwdriver.  "I think there are supposed to be three.  Could you please run over to the other silo and see if the other warhead has one we can steal?"

Jack jogged off in the direction of the other missile bay.  'Red thing'?  Rurick was right.  It was a very good thing they weren't trying to dismantle a live warhead.

The two worked together on the missile's nose cone for another hour and a half before it was pronounced ready.

"You think it'll make it all the way to Moscow?" asked Jack.

Rurick nodded.

"You know, I've gotta admit, I am a little curious why we're blowing up the capitol to save it."

Rurick smiled.  "I was wondering how long you were going to act like you knew what was going on."

He hit a green button on a bright yellow control box that operated an overhead crane.  The de-fanged warhead rose smoothly and easily into the air.

"You remember those records of Russian troop movements you stole from the enemy?"

"Yeah.  They didn't say anything."

Rurick extended an index finger like Socrates giving a lecture to a pupil.  "Ah!  But it's not so much what they did say, but what they didn't say that was important.

"There were no troop movements at all near Moscow.  In fact, everything seemed to be being deployed away from the city.  Another quiz:  What do you make of that?"

Jack thought for a moment.  He was learning an appreciation for the lessons learned from Rurick's pop quizzes, but they still annoyed him.  "Somebody on the inside is clearing the way for the attack."

"Indeed, yes!  And whomever that is, would certainly shut down any objections from a lowly police commandant with a 'pooh-pooh' and a wave of the hand.  But... what happens when a missile (albeit a dud) crashes into the city?"

"They'll know that their defenses weren't there watching!" Jack realized.

"And they'll call the troops back." Rurick added.  He smiled.  "I think you may be getting the hang of this."

Jake smiled too.  It was a hard-won compliment.

The two maneuvered the nose cone into place and bolted it back onto the top of the rocket.  That was it.  They made their way back to the control room with high hopes that their repair job would work out.

They both walked to the main console.  Jack gestured to the chair.  "You'd better drive.  My Russian is only 'passable', remember?"

Rurick entered in a series of commands.  Somewhere in the machinery of the place, pumps engaged and started filling the rocket with fuel and oxidizer.  A countdown clock on the wall began its slow march towards zero.

"What are the coordinates?" asked Rurick.

Jack read them off.  Then Rurick read them back to Jack for safety.  All were correct.  Rurick hit [Enter].

"That's it." said Rurick.

"That's it?"

"Yep.  Nothing else to do but wait for the boom."

Both men jumped visibly as a set of heavy iron shutters dropped down in front of the window.

Jack glanced at the countdown and back at his watch.  He prayed that the rocket would go off.  He prayed that the Russians would take the hint.  He prayed the thing wouldn't land on anybody.  He prayed for a hundred other things all at once.

Then there was the tremendous roar of a thousand muscle cars red-lining at once.

The rocket was away.


~56~
Any Last Words

Wendell, Jane, and Misty floated amid the body of bold Atlantican warriors as the threat loomed nearer.  They were forced to abandon their impromptu repair shop when the cloud of poison reached it.  

The poison-saturated submarines continued slowly tightening their snare.  It wasn't clear whether Enzo was moving so slow to make the trap perfectly inescapable, or whether he was a sadistic housecat, having fun toying with its prey.

After so far they'd come and so hard they'd fought, it seemed a pity for it all to end like this.  The sadness of death was far outweighed by that of failure as the Sea Dragon subs gradually bore down on them.

"Well, since we're about to die here, anybody got any last confessions?" Wendell jested, trying to make light of the situation, and totally failing.

Jane bit her lip.

"I have one." she said at last.

"What?  Really?" asked Wendell, surprised to actually get a taker.

"Never mind him.  You go ahead and say whatever you want." said Misty comfortingly.

"Thanks.  It's just my family--"

"But you said you didn't want to."

"No, it's okay, I..."

"No, you don't have to--"

"I want to.  I do.  It's okay, Misty.  Really."

"O-okay."

Jane sighed and began, "It's just that, you know, I've got this 'problem'.  I mean, they call it Aspergers Syndrome, but..."

Misty placed her hand on Jane's back.  Her friend took great comfort from that, and continued less haltingly.

"Well, I've had it all my life, and nobody really understands what it's like.  Sometimes just little things get to me:  a clothes tag that won't lay right, the way my hair falls, even wearing jeans like the other kids.  (In Kansas, EVERYBODY wears jeans.  I can't stand jeans!  They just irritate my legs something fierce.)  But my mom, she doesn't get it at all.  Every year for school, it's like, 'Janey, I got you a new pair of jeans, dear'.  She just doesn't understand!"

"And your father?"

"Oh, him.  He's all, 'Get over it.  Get yer chores done.'" Jane mimicked.  "School was even worse.  So one day, everything exploded.  I just couldn't cope with it anymore.  I got in a big fight with the parents and ran awa--"  The last word trailed off into a squeak, leaving the rest of the thought drifting in the sea between them.

A long silence prevailed, in which the cool trails drizzling down her cheeks, Jane told herself, could not possibly be tears.  Mask must be leaking, that's all.  She was determined not to let it get to her, therefore it couldn't be tears.  Nope.  Not tears.  Not her.  Not miss independent.

But she couldn't hide her sorrow away this time.  She couldn't even wipe the tears away from behind her face mask.  She looked up to see what kind of horrified reaction would be on her friends' face.  In their eyes instead, she saw only compassion.

With a sniff, she concluded, "You guys don't know what it's like -- always being the odd one out."

Wendell snorted back a laugh.

"You want to talk about misfits, you sure joined the right group." Misty explained softly.

"Yeah, I wonder if Michael goes for all the weirdos on purpose." Wendell mused.   "Like he thinks he's Professor Xavier, assembling a group of mutants."

"Wendell." Misty interrupted.

"Yeah?"

"Turn off the cartoons and speak English, please."

"'K, well, it's like he doesn't try to work around our weirdness like it's a handicap or anything like that.  Rather, it's more like he sees it our own individual super-power or something."

Jane gave a soft laugh.  "You want to eat us out of this one, Wendell?" 

He grinned.

"There.  That's better." Misty gave her friend a hug.

Jane looked up at the subs and ghasped at how close they'd come.  Their final moment was now at hand!

Just as the defenders were about to be mowed down by the deadly line, a reverberating clang and then a lesser one shook the world around them, crushing their guts and sending them tumbling in all directions like tenpins.

Jane knelt on all fours on the sandy seafloor, trying to regain her senses.  At first she thought they'd been hit by something, but no, as far as anyone could tell, the SSN's had no remaining ability to shoot torpedoes.  Her head swiveled dizzily around, searching for the enemy.

The sub that was initially hit remained tumbling in a barrel roll with an enormous dent in its side.  It had knocked into a second one which was also damaged and even forced a third to take emergency evasive maneuvers and break the line.

The ramming device seemed to be an all new sub with oriental markings.  It cruised domineeringly over the top of the damaged ones.

Somewhere in its unseen bridge, Ms McCready shouted, "Wahooo!" like a Wyoming cowboy just off her first bronc' ride.

The ship's Captain was less thrilled, steadying himself by clutching a bank of controls and rattling off some gibberish in Korean.  Probably something to the extent, "What the, [explicative], [explicative] have you done to my [explicative] ship!??"

"Potong Ku, you're gettin' soft in your old age, buddy." chided McCready.

What old favors Ms McCready was able to call in to gain command of an important vessel like that - from a traditionally non-friendly nation, no less - was something that the others never found out.  The fact that a sub with that much firepower was in waters so far from home to begin with?  That was no less classified.

Jane and her team, of course, saw none of what went on inside.  They did, however, realize what quarter the help came from when they heard the voices of Michael and Melinda Moast over their moast_net communicators.

"What are we up against here?" came the no-nonsense voice of Michael Moast.

"We're still a little pinned down here, chief." Wendell spoke for the group.  "Anything you could do about those minisubs flanking us?"

"Rodger that."

Due to dive gear and the rapid loss of detail underwater, they didn't know which of the hundred nearby silhouettes were their allies.  All that changed when two figures shouldered some kind of underwater rocket launchers and, with a bright light and a flurry of bubbles, let fly in the direction of a group of Sea Dragon minisubs.  

One projectile exploded against the hull of its target and the other about three feet away.

At first Jane thought the second was a miss, but then she realized that both subs, and even a handful surrounding them, were no longer moving.

The Atlanticans saw it too and needed no second invitation to swim in and finish off the paralyzed squadron of enemies.

"EMP Rockets!" commented Wendell over his radio.

"What?" asked Jane.

"They knock out electronics.  Turn's em into kibbles and bits."

"Wendell!  No time for chatter!  What's going on here?"  The voice of Melinda was good to hear.  It felt like years since they had last heard it.

"Well, let's see...  The army of Atlantis and Captain Nemo are in a desperate battle against the latest in nuclear submarines, and they think Jane's some kind of warlord / hero." said Wendell.

Jane heard it all and could tell that he was trying to push Melinda's buttons.  Now was simply not the time for it.  She cut in, "Okay, it sounds a little insane when you put that way, but it's true, and it actually makes complete sense once you check it out."

"No it doesn't." added Misty softly.

"Anyway," she shot Misty a dirty look and continued, "If you and Michael could to take those EMP bombs and keep knocking out minisubs, that would be great.  Just be careful though, because some of them are ours.  Also, if those things can take out the big subs, that'd be great too -- except be careful not to damage the Starfish (that's the weird one)."

"I think the big ones are too shielded for these to get through, but we'll mop the (sea) floor with those little guys!" said Michael.  He and his wife made for the densest grouping of them at hand.

The pulse weapons made quick work of everything they came near.  Between the two of them, the Korean sub and the Starfish, they were able to cut their way through the ranks of the enemy subs until the last four SSN's retreated, abandoning the battle for good.  Even one of these the Starfish was able to run down and scuttle.

To a man, the enemy SCUBA divers were either trapped or dead.  One impetuous group opted to leave their crippled SSN sub and make one last charge at the Atlanticans.  Their own poison did them in.  Nobody knew whether to credit that last move as bravery, or whether they were more scared of their employer than certain death.

And there it was.  The battle was now over.  The once thriving city was now littered with drifting bits of torn and busted war machines.  As peace and calm regained a foothold, the fish and other sea creatures began to return and feasted on the bodies of the fallen.


~57~
Unwelcome Guests

The massive bulk of the Starfish sailed majestically overhead and slowly lowered itself to a rest in the main street of Atlantis.  It barely fit between the buildings.  A great murmur of concern arose among the people at seeing this.

The parking ticket would have to wait however.  The first order of business was to tow the poison-saturated Sea Dragon subs out of the city.  

Even this was politically ticklish.  The Korean Captain volunteered magnanimously to do the job.  No doubt his intentions were to haul them off to some known location where they could be easily salvaged and have their nuclear reactors dissected for national secrets.  Nemo volunteered as well, for reasons that could only be similar.

Each Captain accused the other of the worst while vehemently denying their own true intentions.  Eventually it was decided that Nemo should tow them all to a steep-sided ocean trench and sink them.  Then Potang Ku would set off a landslide with torpedoes and bury them under a million tons of rubble. 

While this was being carried out, the cleanup of the other major source of poison was contemplated.  It was a daunting task, but Ms McCready came up with an answer.

She discovered a few casks of something called Thermite in the hold of her borrowed naval warship.  It was intended for performing emergency underwater welds on the hull of the ship.  More important to their current needs, however, it was some seriously hot stuff that could easily burn underwater and take nearly anything it touched with it.

The Sea Dragon minisubs were fitted with sprayers to spread the chemical.  It was doused liberally over Atlantis' swaying seaweed fields and the poisonous snails infesting them.  Since they had enough left over, they also coated the remnants of the dome and all of the Sea Dragons' psychotic experiments.

One small spark was all it took, and all evidence of their existence was happily scorched from the history books forever.  (Even as the chapters denied to Atlantis for so long were being rewritten.)

It would be a hard season for the Atlanticans without their crop, but with (literally) plenty of other fish in the sea, they were in no danger of starvation.

The flickering light of their agriculture slowly burning away lent its ambiance to the somber faces of the surviving Atlanticans as they gathered up the remains of their fallen countrymen.  They toted their dead reverently to the burial plot that they prepared on the outskirts of town.

Jane watched spellbound as a pair of weeping women converged on the body of their fallen loved one.  Even the word "fallen" was a strange one down here, for he floated some feet above the seafloor.

Carefully, gingerly, they bound the man's wounds so that they would not stain the water around.  Then, with the same care, they tightly wrapped his arms and legs so they could no longer drift.

With the gentle touch they used, you'd think the poor, vacant body was now more sensitive, rather than less.

Jane didn't think it would be like this.  She'd expected celebration... parades... trumpets.   Instead, everything was cellos and mourning.

Stranger still, even though she'd lost no one in the conflict, she somehow felt even emptier than before.

But she'd done it, hadn't she?  She'd lived out the Queen's vote of trust, and, against all odds, played a key role in freeing her people from the deadly yoke of the Sea Dragons!  And yet...

She paused the thought long enough to watch the mourners tow the body away.

Once they finally diminished beyond sight, she closed her eyes and swallowed hard.

Where was the victory?  She didn't feel like Atlantis' champion.  She felt like the same confused girl who had run away from home a month or two ago.  

All that she'd been through since then - it changed nothing!

There was plenty of contemplation that needed to be done, and plenty of silent time to do it in.

These threefold tasks of cleaning, burning, and burying took an exhausting two days of grueling work.

On the third day, the Korean Captain decided that he had strayed from his assigned mission long enough.  There would be nothing more of strategic interest to he or his country here.  Ms McCready bid him a fond goodbye, thanked him for the favor, and assured him that certain 'mutual friends' would find a way to show their gratitude as well.

That day Jane was asked to give a dedication speech over the burial ground where those brave soldiers who lost their life in freedom's cause were laid to rest.  It turned out much differently than the previous speech for her stage fright was now trumped by compassion.  She didn't know what she was going to say beforehand and didn't remember what she had said afterwards, but suffice it to say it was beautiful and entirely from the heart.  Tears were not left lacking.

She cried again that evening at the special funeral for the Queen of Atlantis.  Although her acquaintance with the woman had been a brief one, Jane felt a great admiration for her.

Those tears, perhaps, might have been better off not shed.  Not for lack of respect of the noble lady, of course, but for the trouble they brought when her esteemed cousin, Captain Nemo saw them fall.  It intrigued her.

On the fourth day after the victory, the Starfish still resided in the middle of the main street, facing directly towards the palace -- not moving one bit!  Cleaning up nuclear subs and bodies and poisons was one thing, but there was one even dirtier bit of cleanup that still needed to be done.  

It was a dirty job that Jane knew she must be a part of, and did not like it one bit!

The time had come.

The Atlantican Generals and the Moast Bible Study swam down the street towards the Starfish's anchorage.  It might as well have been Wyatt Earp's gang on the way to the O.K. Corral.

They made their way through the main hatch and into the stateroom where Nemo's unsavory welcome dinner had been served mere weeks before.  After all that had happened since, the event seemed like ancient history to Jane and the others.

Captain Nemo stood, as usual, sipping her tea.  Her son Sandy, as usual, stood at her heels - more a piece of furniture or a shadow than an entity unto himself.

"To what do I owe the honor?" asked the Captain with shallow, political politeness.

Tension hung heavily in the air.

One of the Generals cleared his throat and delivered their carefully-crafted speech.

"We, the Generals of Atlantis would like to formally thank you for your service by giving your vessel a proper send-off." said the spokesman.  It was a desperate attempt.  The desperation was even more obvious as he said, "What time today to you intend to make way?"

The Captain sipped her tea.  She took a maddeningly long time doing it, too.  This was going to be like convincing a rhinoceros to please, pretty please, get off your foot.

She ignored the General entirely and looked at Jane instead.  "You met my cousin didn't you?"

Jane didn't know what to say to that.  It didn't seem relevant to the conversation at hand.  

She didn't need to say anything, however.  The Captain read her face like a billboard.  "Ah! So you did meet my cousin!"  Nemo raised an eyebrow in what passed for elated surprise on that face.

There was no use denying it now.  "Yes.  Just before she died." admitted Jane.

A guard in the corner stiffened.

Nemo licked her lips.

"Did you see..."  

The Captain fumbled on the words and began again.

"Did she happen to give you anything?" she asked.  "Anything for me, perhaps?" she hastened to add.

"No."

It was a direct, scandalous, bold-faced lie.  The exact polar opposite of the truth.  Unfortunately, she was adept at the skill from all the times she'd lied to her parents.  

There was still a twinge of guilt at the memory.  It was part of what she had run so far to try and get away from.  This attitude was supposed to have stayed behind in Kansas.  

And she really hated the way it felt.

That was something that she knew God was nagging her to deal with.  Just not now.

For her part, Nemo wasn't about to let the matter drop so easily.

"Did she have a... any jewelry of any kind on her?"

"No."

"A ring, perhaps?"  She stared cold daggers at the Generals, daring them to say anything about it.

"No."

"Because what I'm looking for is a ring with a large black stone and with several figures around it..."  She held up her hand traced out the details in the air around her finger as if she already wore it.

"No."

"It was our great grandfather's, you know..."

"Sorry.  Nothing like that."

And now for the dodge.  "If it's as valuable as you say, the Sea Dragons might have taken it."

"No, not - not valuable." Nemo backpedaled hastily.  "Just... sentimental."

Minutes ticked by.

She sighed and took another sip of tea.  

Now Jane consciously realized what was bothering her.  The hand holding the teacup wore the same ring as the one hiding in her pocket.

Jane put on a confrontational front that she hoped would be convincing.  There wouldn't be any better time to play the trump-card she had secreted away.  "Listen.  You promised to return us to the surface.  'Safe and unharmed,' you said.  We defeated the Landies.  Now you keep your end of the deal.  Return us to the surface.  Today."

The Generals eyed one another.  Could Jane really strong-arm Nemo into leaving?  

But then, the Captain really had promised...

Captain Nemo fairly dropped the cup back on its saucer.  Had she the ability to shoot laser beams out of her eyes, Jane and her friends would have been reduced to ash where they stood.  As it was, the angry glare was the worst she could do.  

With her son the First Mate and so many other crewmen looking on, she was forced to keep her word.  It was either give in or be disgraced as a leader.  In short, she was cornered.

"Of course." she acquiesced.  She tried to make it sound casual - like it was silly to even ask something so obvious.  She even gave a nonchalant wave of the hand to go with it.  Nobody bought it.  The Queen of All Oceans would rather have eaten a mouthful of broken glass than give up her hold on the city.

The Bible Study group filed out to another section of the ship to await their return to the world above.  Meanwhile the leaders of Atlantis disembarked the vessel, with no small amount of fawning and handshaking over Jane.

Because of this delay, Jane brought up the rear of the procession.  As she was passing out the door, Nemo grabbed her by the shoulder and offered one parting threat.

"You'd better not be lying to me, little girl..." she hissed.


Much to the relief of the town, the Starfish rose from its foothold and began to move forward.  True to their word, the townspeople lined the street and waved brightly-colored cloths and cheered.  

Whether their jubilation was more to celebrate the Starfish's victory or its leaving was hard to say.  Captain Nemo gritted her teeth.  She felt every ounce of the double-meaning.

The sub rose steadily as it sailed down the main street.  As it cleared the level of the buildings, a procession of re-purposed Sea Dragon minisubs flanked it on each side.  Even that military escort could be taken two ways.  It could be a military salute or it could be a show of strength.

The Captain made a symbolic gesture of her own, circling above the palace twice before vectoring off toward open water.


Jane met up with the others already gathered in the Starfish's mess hall.

"What was all that back there?" asked Michael Moast.

The rest of the group looked like they wanted to know too.  

The Captain would logically be the rightful and legal heir to any of her cousin's effects.  The fact that Jane would lie just to keep a piece pretty jewelry that didn't belong to her seemed... disappointing.  Still, they didn't say anything at the time, which showed that they still posessed some trust in their friend's character.

"I didn't like it.  Something just didn't add up." she replied.

More explanation was clearly needed than that.  Being forced to put that 'something' into words helped the picture to solidify in her own mind.  It came together even as she spoke.

"The Queen of Atlantis said to keep the ring a secret.  Said to keep it a secret from 'her'.  Well, who is 'her'?  Also, she said, 'Atlantis must be free'.  But 'Free', from who?"

" 'Whom'." corrected Wendell unhelpfully.

"This 'her' person, that's 'whom'!" continued Jane.

Misty began to see it too.  "Did you see the Atlanticans reaction when Jane showed them the ring back at the dome?  All of a sudden Jane is like, their big hero.  This thing isn't just something a queen wears.  This is what makes her queen!  That's why Nemo wants it so bad!"

Something about Misty's words nagged at Wendell.  There seemed to be more going on that she wasn't saying.  An 'elephant in the room', so to speak.

Jane nodded.  "The Captain wants to be Queen of Atlantis.  But her cousin didn't want her to be.  She wanted Atlantis to be free.  That was the most important thing to her, even if it meant..."  She let the statement trail off as if hesitant to say it.

Again, something profound went unsaid.

Misty combined her thought with Jane's and came to a conclusion.  "Did you catch what Captain Nemo calls herself?  'The Queen of all Oceans'.  It must gall her to no end that she owns it all except Atlantis, the very crown jewel itself.  Her greatest ambition must be to wear the crown of Atlantis."  And then  she corrected herself,  "Er, I mean, 'ring' of Atlantis."

Now Wendell noticed what the girls were pussy-footing around and jumped into it with both feet.  "Ladies and gentlemen," he announced in the voice of a royal herald, "I give you... Jane Jones, Queen of Atlantis!"  He bowed with a flourish.

Michael and Melinda looked wide-eyed at eachother.  Ms McCready was no less shocked.

Jane only gulped.  All of a sudden she felt the full weight of it.  Sure, she'd just played a key role in assisting the army of Atlantis to victory.  And, of course, she was headed for land, never again intending to return to the strange undersea people.  Still, somehow, the title held a tangible weight to it.


Nemo dropped the team on an uninhabited island near the African coast, not far from where she had picked up Jane originally.  It was painful to the team to have to travel all that way only to have to backtrack again to return to where Michael had left their boat.  But it was his opinion that the Atlanticans needed all the time they could buy for them -- to prepare in case Nemo decided to return.


~58~
Moscow

General Mirkov pulled into his private spot in the Kremlin's VIP parking area.  He was glad that his position allowed him to have such a nice perk.  He'd thought it was silly before now.  But today was the first day he'd driven his brand new American Corvette.  

It had deep purple metal-flake paint and a t-top.

It was an extravagance, sure - certainly one that the lingering vestiges of Communist rule frowned upon.  But Mirkov had been saving every spare ruble.  Between that and his new commission as General of Domestic Defense, he was able to finally finance his dream.

It was a fine machine.  And as an old war dog he'd learned to appreciate a fine machine.

He brushed off a leaf that had audaciously fallen on the hood on the way over.

He smiled.

He sighed. 

Then, reluctantly, he turned and made his way to his office.

It was a hard morning.  He didn't get much paperwork done.  His office window overlooked the parking lot, so he often found himself daydreaming about long rides in the country rather than the details of tanks and budgets and the rising price of armored steel plate.

He tried to force his eyes to look at the report, but they floated back to the car once more as if drawn by a magnet.

Right before his eyes the General saw his precious vehicle suddenly impaled deep into the pavement by a huge nuclear missile.  The rocket shredded itself to bits and let out a billowing fireball of unspent propellant.

The sonic shockwave of it hit the building a split second later, shattered every window in it, and knocked him flat to the floor on his back.

The General lay stunned for a few moments.

He propped himself up on one elbow.  Wide eyes.  Shocked.

As the reaction finally settled in, his gaping mouth slowly closed into gritted teeth.  He was on his feet and storming out, crunching over glass bits and debris.  He had no idea what just happened but somebody was going to pay.  Ooh, how they were going to pay!

Minutes later, the airwaves were jammed solid with traffic as every military unit from bombardier to doorknob-polisher scrambled into high alert.

Two hours later the surprise attack force of the would-be Czar rumbled into Moscow like cows up the ramp to the slaughterhouse.  

Tanks parked in a pre-arranged crossfire formation made mincemeat out of the vehicles and troops.  Primed, and ready surface-to-air missiles tore their helicopters to shreds.  High-alert interceptors blew their missiles out of the sky.

The cleverly designed rebellion was over before it began.


~59~
Odious Oaths

Somewhere in the streets of Hong Kong, three perilous-looking characters sat at a dimly-lit barroom table.

To an observer, two of the men looked strangely similar to one another.  Same build.  Same hair.  Same evil steel-grey eyes.  The lone difference was that one was dignified and dressed in rich Chinese robes.  The other, clad in Euro-American garb, had clearly given the bartender a fair amount of business this evening.

"Fong," the sauced man slurred the name, "you're one lucky punk, ya know that?  Here, you get all the Orient handed to you on a silver platter and what do I get?  I get splashed in the drink by some kinda 'Rambo Scouts' kids.  I barely escaped with my life from Atlantis and those brats."

The third man had been silent through the evening so far.  His eye patch and grizzled appearance spelled nothing but lethal.  He frowned energetically, glaring at his shot glass like it was filled with the most vial stuff ever concocted.  It probably was, but the bitterness causing his expression was that of anger and vengeance.

At the drunken man's complaint, the one-eyed man came to life.

He pounded the table with force.

"That girl." he spat venom.  "I'm gonna find 'er.  And when I do..."

He began to describe in great detail all of the ghastly ways of delivering pain that his vile mind could conceive.

The other two ignored him.  It was his only topic of conversation of late and they'd heard it a time or twelve already.

"Enzo," said the robed man.  His New Jersey accent didn't match his clothing at all.  
"You might have been better off drownin'.  Draco ain't happy with you losing half his fleet and the underwater city, but he's downright hoppin' mad about the poison.  He wants to move ahead with the plan.

"My part is done."  There was no shortage of personal pride in the statement.  "But now there's no bomb, is there?  What're we gonna do now that there's no bomb, Enzo?"

"I don't know...  I don't know..." he grabbed his head and shook it as if trying to shake some sense out of his foggy brain.

"Well, good thing for you Draco does."  He slid an envelope from his robe and flicked it in Enzo's face with two fingers.

Enzo would normally of beat Fong's teeth out of his face for a rude gesture like that, but right now he was too curious what the letter said.  Curious why Draco was giving him new orders rather than a knife in the back.

He tore open the envelope using his finger as a letter opener and proceeded to read its contents.  A wicked smile slowly spread across his face.

Meanwhile, the one-eyed man continued to rant on.  He was, of course, the very one that Jane had caused to be stabbed in the face in the Atlantis dome's submarine bay.  That made her the sole object of the thug's constant bloodthirsty thoughts.

It was strange to think that only six months earlier, Jane was just a nobody from an unknown town in the middle of nowhere.  But now there were foiled mercenaries plotting her tortuous demise!

At that exact moment, the one-eyed man wasn't the only guy with Jane on his mind.  There was another who was busily plotting intentions far, far different.  And yet, somehow, no less ominous.

Half a world away and beneath the unfathomable depths of the sea, Sandy Nemo, defender of the deep, heir of the Nautilus, and Prince of All Oceans made himself a vow of his own.  His late adolescent mind decided that he had found his future Queen in none other than the humble Janet Jones from landlocked Kansas.

The idea sounded almost laughable, but his earnestness was not to be underestimated.  Sandy knew the language of the fish and of the waves.  He could hear the crash of the surf on any distant shore.  The ocean was his.  It belonged to him.  Should the fair beauty dip even a toe into the salty sea, he was confident that he would feel its ripple.

And the next time the sea closed over her head, she would never again return to land.  That, he vowed.


~60~
Cheapskate

The chartered seaplane hit the rolling surface of the waves with a splash of spray and glided to a stop next to the streamlined yacht.

All shook hands with the African bush pilot and thanked him for his services.  The Moasts thanked him even further with an obscenely large check.  His bright white teeth spread into a big grin.

The group climbed aboard with the help of a rope ladder tossed over the port side.  

Michael and Melinda were greeted warmly by their old friend, the Captain of the Cheapskate.  He had never met Ms McCready, nor Jane and Misty.  Wendell?  He wasn't entirely sure whether he was happy to see him again or not.

Once aboard, Ms McCready went straight for the galley.  She emerged back on deck a moment later with a loaf of bread, peanut butter, grape jelly, and a long knife.  The ship employed a cook, but she insisted on doing it herself.  She was back to the sweet, tired old lady routine.  Jane still wasn't buying it, but after the long flight, wasn't about to look a gift PB&J in the eye.

Dr Puttery was dialed in over the Moast's satellite grid and placed on speaker.

"I'm sorry I couldn't reach Jack or Kurt," he apologized,  "Eastern Russia seems to rely on an internet connection constructed mainly from borscht and reindeer tallow."  He spoke the insult as if a slow connection speed was the true distinguishing mark of barbarians.

He continued, "I'll forward a recording on to them, but I was just too excited to wait!  You actually found Atlantis, you say?"

Instead of answering his question directly, Michael thought it would be more efficient to recount the girls' and Wendell's adventures from the beginning.  This, they did to the best of their memory.  Dr Puttery seemed anxious when he heard about the photos of beautiful but deadly poisonous sea snails, but was again forced to leave his curiosity hanging for later.  

He, of course, flipped his wig over the artifacts Jane had found, but decided for the third time not to interrupt the narrative.  (Everyone could tell that the suspense was eating him up inside.)

The adventures of the three were described up until that very moment, but what had happened above the waves still remained a mystery to them.

"So how about you guys?  What shady shenanigans have the rest of you been up to?" asked Wendell.

Melinda Moast caught a fleeting expression out of the corner of her eye.  She instantly zeroed in on Ms McCready like a rabbit in a cross-hair.  She frowned and said softly,  "You've been drinking again."  The knife clattered to the deck and Ms McCready's head flopped into her arms in incriminated despair.  

One drink.  Six days ago.  How Melinda could do that was an eternal mystery to her.

"Well, there go the sandwiches." observed a disheartened Wendell.

That eased the tension a bit, but Jane opted to jump in and change the subject entirely.  "I'm a little curious.  How did you even find us, Michael?"

"Easy." he replied.  "We simply followed your trail."

"Trail?"

"Of course.  First, there was the message and photos you uploaded from the...  'Peanut', I guess you're calling it.  That gave us the basic heading.  And then later there was the beacon that you marked Atlantis itself with."

"Beacon?" she asked, confused.

Wendell knew what he meant, but Jane figured it out before he could speak.  In effect she answered her own question,  "Of course!  The life raft's emergency beacon!"  

"Yep." agreed Michael.  "In fact, Captain Ajax here was already parked right on top of the city by the time we even arrived."

The Captain of the Cheapskate smiled modestly.

Wendell St Frank, who'd been on the trail of Atlantis for so long - even to the point of driving his fellow adventurers past the brink - had to know one thing.

"So Atlantis... where was it, finally?"

"You're not going to believe this!" said Michael, pointing off into the horizon.

There, on the very edge of unaided vision, was a piece of land.

"England." he smiled.

Wendell gawked.  "You mean..."

"Yep.  One of the greatest mysteries of humanity was right here beneath one of the world's busiest shipping lanes the whole time."

The realization was flabbergasting, but certainly not what Dr Puttery cared about.  He had watched the location parked on his mechanical display screen for days, and no longer found it newsworthy.  No, there were other questions on his mind.

"If this concludes your tale, might we please move on to the artifacts now?"  Dr Puttery was beside himself in curiosity.

Having not seen the undersea city himself, he was still on the skeptical side that it was, in fact, the legendary lost city, or perhaps something else.  

Michael Moast first described the brass pieces in detail, even uploading a picture or two.  

The Antiques Roadshow assessment was that they were all interesting plumbing parts and were done in a variation of late 1800's styling.  The thread pattern matched the date as well.  They logically had more to do with the more recent inhabitants than the ancient world.  They had little worth besides their weight in scrap.  

Or, perhaps, they might be of value to a Jules Verne fan, on the offhand chance that they might have once been handled by the original Captain Nemo.  If one really let one's imagination run wild, they might even be derelict parts of the Nautilus herself!  (But there was, of course, no way of proving that either way.)

When they came to the broken pot the tone of the conversation changed entirely.

"Is that so?" said Dr Puttery with a faraway tone in his voice.  "Hard to appraise the authenticity of it over the phone, but... hold on... I haven't been able to find our original Atlantis pot around here anywhere.  I think Wendell may have taken it with him.  If so, could you compare the two for me?"

Wendell realized that, though it had all worked out for the best, he was still in more than a little bit of trouble over his original treasure hunting escapade.  He decided that prompt cooperation on his part might help to lessen the consequences.

"Yeah, I've got it in my cabin.  Just a sec..."

He reemerged a few minutes later carrying the old pot fragment carefully wrapped in a Snoopy beach towel.  It was a technically effective way of protecting it from damage, but the Moasts were still uneasy at seeing that method of carrying around such a valuable artifact.

With a concerned expression, Michael unwrapped it.  He compared the pot shard found by Jane in Atlantis to the one from California to see if they were possibly from the same culture.  The black silhouetted image of a woman in Greek garb did indeed seem to look the same between the two fragments.  

Slowly, and with a slight nervous tremble, the hands holding the two shards brought them together.  The pieces fit perfectly with not even a single chip missing!  These were not only the same kind of pot, but indeed, two parts of the exact same pot! 

They all contemplated the item in Michael's hands...  Broken in the last moments of the old Atlantis Empire, and discarded as junk with the building of the new Atlantis Empire.  For the first time in 2,500 years it was whole again.  For the first time in 2,500 years it stood in the sun.

"That is just unreal!" said Jane.

"What's going on?" asked Dr Puttery over the phone.

"Amazing bit of Providence." agreed Michael.

"What's going on??" came the voice with more eagerness.

"I can't believe it!" added Misty.

Dr Puttery seemed about to burst!

Michael took pity and relieved his curiosity.

"You're kidding me!  That's impossible!" shouted Puttery.

Michael smiled.  "What do you say Puttery?  Has she impressed you yet?"

For a minute he didn't know what Michael was talking about.  Then he remembered his earlier words, that Jane would have to do something to "impress me", to be considered a member of the group.  Now he found himself thoroughly abashed because of it.

The line was silent for a long time.

Somewhere on the other side of the globe Dr Puttery raised his glass in salute.  "She has indeed, Michael.  Hats off to you Miss Jones!"

Then, in way of apology, he added, "Forgive my rudeness in having doubted you."  And he meant it sincerely.

Having resolved that issue, Michael now had is own concerns to voice.  Things more immediately urgent than both Dr Puttery's scientific curiosity and his personal character judgments.

Michael's tone turned a little grim.  "You know what this means, right?"

"They both came from the same place." said Misty.  Her happy voice was opposite enough from Michael's that there must be something more to it than that.

Jane hit it.

"The underground antiques smuggling operation." she realized.  "We've got a direct link between that and the chemical weapons production ring  -- The Sea Dragons."

"Exactly!" agreed Michael.  "And I wouldn't be surprised to learn that the one was helping to finance the other."

Dr Puttery flew into a coughing fit on the other end of the line.  "Sea Dragons?  Did you just say... Sea Dragons?"

"Yes.  That's what they called themselves.  Why?" asked Michael Moast.

"Because that phrase is all over the place in a coded message that Jack intercepted in Siberia!"

Wendell contributed his own hypothesis.  "So... this really isn't over then, is it?  

"I mean, yeah, we stopped them today.  So did Jack.  But we sure didn't capture their leader or find out who they were working for.  The powers that made this happen are still out there somewhere.  And something tells me they're not the sort of folks to give up and run home crying to mama over a little setback or two."

Michel nodded.

Misty tried to inject a little bit of optimism.  "At least we've got a trail to follow now."

It was, indeed, true.  No doubt Michael Moast would make secret agents around the globe very happy when he turned this information over to them.

Wendell sat on a deck chair across from Michael carefully watching his expression.  The gears were turning in his analytical mind, strategizing away on what would have to happen next.  He waited for some change.  Some sign.  Something that would indicate a decision as to their next move.

As he stared at Michael's face a sudden thought struck Wendell like a lightning bolt straight to the funny bone.  He tried to hold it in, but it was as futile as trying to hold back the tide itself.  He began to laugh hysterically.  Nobody else knew why.

His index finger, shaking in mirth, pointed at the pot.  "Wait till Michael gets the bill for that!"

Misty and Ms McCready caught on and instantly joined in the laughter.  

Michael and his wife tried to frown but couldn't.

Even Dr Puttery could be heard chuckling over the satellite link.

Now only Jane was left in the dark.

"I don't get it." she said blandly, feeling like an outsider for the first time in a long while.

It took a few more seconds before Misty caught her breath enough to speak between belly laughs.

"H-he bid 300 grand for the other half!" and returned to laughing.

"The small half!" added Wendell.

Jane smiled and blushed slightly.

"Oh, just something I found in the garbage..."

They all now laughed together as the Cheapskate drifted off into the sunset, towards the much missed shores of the good old USA.


~61~
Assisted Living

The Laurel Ridge Assisted Living home stood on a rocky bluff overlooking the sea. 

Its peachy stucco and terracotta tile roof made it look like a forgotten Spanish hacienda, or a home for some pretentious California multimillionaire.  

It had actually spent time as both of those things.

Now, however, the millionaires it housed were those whose years pushed them past the age where they could live alone anymore.  Many such people had no families, and so, bought the comfort and care they needed at the establishment.

The opulence and size of the place made the transition easy for most.  In fact, if one could think of the many other rooms as being unoccupied, the place would quite remind them of home.

Indeed, each of the occupants preferred to look right through one another as if there was nobody in the place but themselves and their servants.  The lifetime of isolation continued on, even in the midst of company.

Naturally, the overhead of maintaining the place was kept as unobtrusive as possible.  Even the front entryway was designed to look like a den or library complete with ornate carved bookshelves and books chosen for their decorative appeal rather than their contents.  The scene all came together so cleverly that it almost seemed to scream for the existence of the manager's stout desk and green glass banker's lamp.

And indeed, the female manager sat at such a desk, now flipping through a stack of paperwork, searching for whatever bit business was being worked on today.

The front door opened and shut.

"Excuse me?" said a man's voice.

She began replying before even looking up, "Good afternoon sir, and who would you like to...  Jack!"  Once she'd torn herself from her paperwork she immediately recognized the face.  It was more dirty and jet-lagged than normal, but his handsome features were unmistakable.

She glanced at a calendar out of the corner of her eye and let the facts dictate what she'd say next.  "Looks like you and your Bible Study aren't scheduled to play again until next Wednesday, so I assume you're here for your friend, then?"

Jack nodded.

"Follow me, please.  I'll take you to him."

He followed her ridiculously tight skirt all the way down to the end of a hallway.  Most men would have found it alluring, but Jack was exhausted and found that the tight constriction of her legs made her walk like a penguin.  An annoyingly slow penguin at that!

"I think it's really sweet how you two hit it off." she observed nosily, "In fact, I think you're about the only person the mean old codger actually likes.  But I guess I probably shouldn't talk about a tenant that way."

Jack only grunted.  Didn't this woman ever shut up?

"Yes, there he is." she said at last, opening a french door that led onto the balcony.

And there he was, indeed.  The gaunt man was standing in front of his wheelchair and leaning on a marble rail.  He looked lost in thought as he gazed out over the sea, which churned and roiled like his turbulent memories of the past.

The breakers crashing against the rock far below stirred the wind into strange eddies which tossed his white hair to and fro chaotically.

"Dennis!" complained the woman, "What are you doing out of your chair?"

He only gave her a sideways glance and ignored the scolding entirely.  

That was the difficulty about working in this place.  You were expected to keep the people healthy and safe, but they were so headstrong and stubborn from living life according to their own rules, that, good luck with that!

She sighed and was about to try again, but he spotted Jack first.

"Thank you.  You may leave us." he told her without turning again.

She sighed.  Was she a caregiver or a servant?  Could somebody please answer that?

The woman decided to do as she was told, but didn't leave without giving Jack one last flirtatious smile.   He only responded with a gruff, "Thanks."

Jack walked to the railing and leaned on it next to the other man.  For a long time, neither spoke.  They just stood and watched the sea.

Its turbulence spoke to both of them in the same way.  Perhaps that was why they got along so well with eachother.

At length the elder man broke the silence.  His eyes sparkled with interest mixed with a sprinkling of regret.  "You know, if I could wish for just one thing, I'd wish that I could be out there with you.  But I guess the world belongs to the young." he sighed.

After a moment of self-pity he slapped the railing with both palms.  "But never mind that!

"So, young pup, ready to tell me all about your latest amazing adventure?"

Jack smiled.

He nodded.


~62~
New York

Jane marveled at her.  The woman stood proud and tall, her expression immobile.  Ever watching.  Ever vigilant.  Ever looking...  directly over at Brooklyn??  (Which, in some ways, seemed to suck a certain amount of the dignity out of it.)

The woman was, of course, the Statue of Liberty herself.  

The others were oblivious to the landmark, having seen it many times before on their various globetrotting journeys.  To Jane, however, it was all so very new and exciting.  She took many pictures of the statue, fire boats, the New York skyline...  

It seemed strange that these typical tourist shots should live on her camera right next to international terrorists, sea people, and the most important archeological discovery of the century.

Misty watched her friend experiencing the sights for the first time.  Though Jane had handled herself marvelously, she was still such a beginner at this game.  For all she knew, the adventure was over and done with - like a movie plot that had run its course and awaited only the closing credits to roll.  

Her friend didn't yet realize that in real life, things were never that cut-and-dry.  Solving one mystery led to another, adventure begat adventure, and one was never, ever, out of danger.

Misty shook off the thought and then looked around at the harbor, trying to recall what it was like for her the first time she had visited the place.  She couldn't seem to make it materialize.

The Cheapskate had opted to ignore her home port of Key West and decided to make directly for New York instead.  

Not only was it much closer, but Dr Puttery had an acquaintance at Columbia University who was pretty fired up over the news of the ancient pottery.  Dr Kyle was the man's name.  He even offered to drop everything on the spot and charter a helicopter to fly out directly to the Cheapskate.

It was a silly gesture.  The Cheapskate was originally designed to allow a helicopter to land on its aft deck, but the helipad was presently unusable.  It had been altered into a resting place for The Peanut, complete with a boom crane to lower it over the side.  All of this would be in the way of a helicopter.

No,  the artifact had already waited 2,500 years.  Two or three days more was little in comparison.

The Captain of the Cheapskate navigated into the shipping terminal that the Moasts owned and made directly for the center of it.  The dockmaster commanded the bustling scene from a tall central tower like an air traffic controller.  At the base of this was a small dock floating low in comparison to the main wharf.  It was designed more for individual-sized boats like the Cheapskate, rather than the massive container ships and tankers lined up in each direction.

The expert crew tied off the Cheapskate quickly, but nobody got a chance to disembark before Dr Puttery and Dr Kyle were clambering over the side to meet them.

Michael anticipated this and had warned his team to expect a hasty departure.  Their gear was already packed and on deck.  Jane's bag was in her hand and her camera around her neck.

Their readiness was appreciated.  In fact, the two impatient scientists already had a cab waiting on the wharf.  Jane was surprised to see a wharf so big that the car could drive right out to the ship.  The wonder was replaced with a greater one when she noticed that rail lines ran the length of it as well.  Entire freight trains full of heavy containers could be loaded or unloaded directly shipside.  The massive scale of the operation was hard to digest.

Jane's head was too abuzz with the many sights and sounds of the city to remember much of the trip to the University.

The cab pulled up next to Dr Kyle's research facility, a stately old brick building.  Students crowded the sidewalks of campus on their way to various classes as Jane and the others made their way toward the research building's entrance.

Without warning, somebody smacked Jane hard from behind sending her flailing towards the ground, arms flung toward the sky.  It could have been a random, rude accident except for the fact that she never hit the pavement.  Instead a beefy arm grabbed her.  It seemed familiar, somehow.  She tried to scream, but the assailant's opposite hand clamped down hard over her mouth.

He took two steps to carry her off when a second pair of hands grabbed her feet.  The going got faster.

Misty was the first to notice her disappearance and saw her being spirited away into the crowd.  With a single word, the entire team was in hot pursuit.

Jane bit hard on the hand and tried to scream.  Not a bad plan generally, but this time it backfired.  She felt a heavy crack to her head.  Her vision exploded with stars, which slowly faded away into black.

She never knew what a valiant chase her friends put up on her behalf.  Even after her limp body was thrown across the lap of a waiting motorcyclist, they continued to pursue until their legs could carry them no more.

With heavy hearts, the Bible Study returned back to where Dr Kyle was still waiting.  His face was now a ghostly pale.

"What's the matter?" asked Misty.

"I-I'm sorry." he replied.  "They said they were with the government.  Had signed papers and everything..."

"Who did?" asked Michael.

"Those two men.  They asked if anybody had photos of the Sea Dragon terrorists.  I mentioned your friend did and they said they wanted to talk to her."

"You what!??"  Dr Puttery was outraged.  Yet the question proved to be rhetorical because he was too busy decking Dr Kyle to wait for an answer.

Dr Kyle instinctively fought back.

The steady flow of students stopped bustling to and fro and instead gathered to look at the two Doctors wrestling and slugging it out on the wet campus grass.  They didn't know the nature of the dispute, nor really know how to react.  If it was two freshmen, they'd be cheering and placing bets by now.  But two (assumed) professors?  It might as well have been the Queen of England in a drunken bar fight.

Pushing her own frustration aside, Melinda stepped in and tried to pry the two apart.  "That's enough!  It was a stupid mistake, but fighting about it won't bring Jane back."

Puttery rose first and offered a hand to Dr Kyle.  Muttered apologies passed both directions.

Melinda's hand shot out and caught the baggy sweater of Misty as she charged for Dr Kyle like a crazed bobcat.  "Let it go, Misty.  Every minute we waste here, puts Jane farther away."

Misty relaxed.  A little.

But how were they ever going to find Jane in a city of eight and a half million people?


~63~
Regatta

The invading warships sailed up Tokyo Harbor as bold as you please.

They passed directly over the historical spot where the Japanese signed the declaration of surrender to China and the other allied powers, ending World War II.

This time their millennia-old rival wouldn't be so easily turned away from the gates.

The captain of the destroyer Nanking surveyed it all through his binoculars.  Throngs of people lined the docks and crowded the shore as far as the eye could see.  A little girl innocently smiled and waved at the huge ship.  Though she couldn't have seen him, he smiled and waved back in malicious glee.

"Ready all guns." he said calmly.

The Bosun added his own urgency to the message which he called out over the boat's P.A. system.

The rest of the armada did the same, awaiting the electronic sync signal from the commander of the flagship to each launch its salvo in unison.

It silently came.  All at once the multitude of terrible warships erupted in a shockwave of simultaneously firing ordinance.  

The shells each shrieked skyward, trailing a plume of red smoke.  As they reached their zenith above the city they all exploded in a shower of brightly-colored confetti.  A squadron of Chengdu J-20 fighters shot overhead, trailing their own decorative smoke along with golden fireworks that crackled and popped like gold stars against the solid red backdrop.

Children squealed with delight as they ran around in circles in the falling confetti as if it were raining candy.  Bands struck up the Chinese national anthem to welcome the parade of flag-strewn warships.  Everyone clapped and cheered and partied for the better part of a week.

How kind indeed, for Mr Fong, the new Paramount Leader of China, to have sent his own flotilla to ring in this important Japanese holiday.

Indeed, Fong was already a national hero in China.  He was a cruel dictator, of course, but apart from the lack of zealous, blood-stirring slogans, that wasn't too far removed from communist rule.  

In fact, many people found a newfound level of freedom under his reign.  The communists demanded at least some sense of social order and enforced it with an iron fist.  Fong couldn't care less.  He would much rather have the national police rumbling across Asia in tanks than at home spying on their nextdoor neighbors. 

And if an entire province or two decided to stop making cheap dollar-store trinkets in favor of full-scale drug production?  Why should he bother to stop them?  In fact, that'd mean higher GDP and more product for his smuggler friends to sell.  

On the other end of the transaction, if his enemies wound up with more addicts burglarizing neighborhoods and overdosing in the streets, so much the better!

Such were the callous thoughts of Mr Fong.

But as for the Japanese, they only saw and heard of Fong the great reformer.  Perhaps now even the long-standing enmity between the two nations could finally cease!  The news media said as much.  

They'd said it because the leadership told them to say it.  And the leadership told them to say it because they were being blackmailed by Fong.

In any case, tighter relations between the two countries wasn't a lie.  It just meant something far, far different from what the people were led to believe.

As the week's end approached the cheerful celebration slowly drifted into dumbstricken shock as it dawned on both people and politicians alike that Fong's ships had no intention of leaving.  Yes, even the crooked bureaucrats who had welcomed Fong with open arms suddenly saw the foolishness of their ways.  

It was far too late for any of that, of course.  The battleships now held the capitol city hostage with a couple of other kinds of smoke bombs that the crowd would enjoy infinitely less.  

Sadly for Fong and his cohorts, the racks that should have housed canisters of mollusk venom stood completely empty in the ship's hold.  If it came down to a general massacre he would have to resort to the regular, and far less efficient, forms of killing.

But all of that was a moot point anyway.  It was already over.  Japan had opted not to rebuild its standing army after World War II restrictions expired, and now they had a knife to their jugular with no offensive means of retaliation.

Those nations bound and sworn to defend her, now did so only with paperwork and hollow words.

Thus, the island nation was effectively checkmated in three moves.

Without a single drop of blood let, nor a cherry blossom out of place, so fell mighty Japan.

...The second world superpower in as many weeks to slip compliantly into Fong's open and ready clutches.


~64~
Warehouse

By the time the fog lifted, Jane found herself inside a long warehouse lined with crates.  Dust drifted in the air.  In some ways it seemed like the Blevens loading dock.  

Yet there were significant differences as well.  The scale was magnitudes larger but more than that, there was also the smell.  Blevens' smelled mostly like leather and cardboard.  This smelled like a hundred different things competing with one another.  Some were pleasant, some not, but all were strange. 

Jane tried to move her arms, but couldn't.  She realized she was strapped to something.  A chair? she guessed.

"Well, if it ain't sleepin' beauty?" said a gravelly voice from behind her.

The speaker walked into view.  A rough and ugly thug with the fire of unrestrained hatred burning in his eye.  'Eye'?  Yes indeed, for there was only one!

Jane realized with dread that he was none other than the Sea Dragon she'd so narrowly escaped from in their Atlantis control center.

"Forgive me for not awakening you with a kiss."  He bowed in a mocking imitation of Prince Charming.  "Maybe my men can arrange some of that for later."

From the surrounding shadows, voices hooted and whistled and cheered.

Jane glanced around the room, squinting into the darkness.  There were twenty or thirty of them leering back at her in anticipation.  None looked familiar.  They could be Sea Dragons, or just random gang members recruited on the spot for a quick grand and a promise of a good time.

"First off though, I'll take that."  He didn't bother to take her camera strap off over her head, but simply grabbed it and yanked straight towards him.  Her head whipped forward with the pull.  The strap grated its way across her neck and the back of her skull.

"Nice camera!"  Again, he was mocking.  "Betcha got lots of great photos of us, didn't cha?"

Jane shook her head.

"Aw, sure ya did.  Plenty of smilin' faces.  Plenty of shots of our operation.  Plenty of..."  He dropped the delicate camera on the concrete floor. "... evidence."

Jane heard the putter of an engine being started.  Then she heard a wrench fall in the distance.  Then she heard the familiar, beep... beep... beep of a forklift.

"No!" she shouted.  She tried to put all the emotion she could manufacture into the desperate cry.

She thrashed at her restraints, watching helplessly as the forklift advanced ever closer.

The camera's waterproof casing was supposedly 'impact resistant'.  Apparently the warranty stopped short of being driven over by a five ton piece of warehouse machinery.  The camera split in two like a walnut under the solid wheels of the heavy forklift.  The metal beast felt nothing and kept driving forward, smashing electronics and glass and plastic all into pea-sized bits.

The one-eyed man was evidently taking great pleasure in Jane's anguished cries.

After the forklift passed, he crouched down and spread out the fragments with his hand.  Spying what he was looking for, he picked it up and walked over to Jane.  She could see that he held between his thumb and forefinger what remained of the memory card.

"And if you still had any hopes for this thing..."  He opened his mouth to reveal rank, half-rotted teeth.  He dropped the memory card inside, chewed carefully, and swallowed.

"Now that we got that settled, I b'lieve we have the matter of payback to discuss."  He cracked his knuckles and took a step towards her.

"Avast there One-Eyed Willy!"
